Hi, I’m Eben Pagan. Welcome to the Guru Master Class Management Summit. In this program, we’re going to
be talking about how to manage an information product in Guru Business. And in our other programs in the
other pillars of the Guru Mastermind Program we’ve talked about marketing, we’ve talked about sales, we’ve
talked about creating products, we’re talking about conversion. We’re talking about very important things,
creating products, and getting customers.
Well, another important pillar, really the fourth pillar of growing a successful Guru or Information Product
Business, is the management of that business. The management of that business. And it’s an area that’s both
highly important, and it’s also something that’s very neglected by most information product Gurus. And the
neglect of your business, the neglect of the structure that supports all of these great things in your business can
lead to some big problems.
In this program, we’re going to talk about three particular areas of management that are the highest leverage
and most important. The first one is time management. The second one is people management. And the third
area is business management. And as the program progresses, you’ll see how all of these elements go together.
But to start with, I’d like to brighten up your day by sharing an insight that I’ve had after many years of studying
business, and that is that we humans are wired to fail at business.
The evidence is almost conclusive that we humans are wired to fail at business. Now, how could this be? What’s
the evidence? Well, the evidence is that most businesses fail. Michael Gerber, the author of the book, The
E-Myth, which everyone should read, it’s a very important book, he says that 80 percent of businesses fail in
the first five years, and another 80 percent of the businesses that live fail in the next five years, 96 percent in the
first ten years. Most projects fail, most software development installations fail, most mergers and acquisitions
fail. It’s really a miracle that business even works at all with all the failure that’s happening out there.
And so after looking at this for a long time and saying how could it be possible because business, when you
really get down to it, it’s challenging and it’s complex and so forth, but it really isn’t that hard. It’s not really
that big of a deal. So how is it that it fails so often, and so many aspects of business fail? And the conclusion
that I’ve come to and the realization is that we are biologically and culturally wired for failure in business. It’s
not the way that we come equipped into the business world.
Now, I use this term failure very loosely, and as you’ll find out as you get to know me a little bit better, and you
get into the training, that I don’t really “believe” in failure. I think of failure as a learning experience. I think
that the concept of failure is something that we’ve invented. But just for fun, go with me down this pathway
because I think it can lead to maybe some insights that can help you really create a lot of success.
So the question I have is, if we’re wired for business failure, and all of the evidence shows that we’re wired for
failure, and programmed for failure, how is it that some businesses succeed? How is it that some businesses
succeed so well? How do some perform outside of kind of all statistical expectation? How could that be? What
is the difference, and more specifically, what is the difference that makes the difference?
If you study a bunch of businesses, and you say what do all of these businesses have in common, let’s say that
you take companies that are doing $100 million or more per year in business, and you say okay, let’s go figure
out what they have in common, which is what a lot of kind of business and management guru types do, you’ll
find that they all have in common that they employ people.
So principle number one could be, well, you must employ people, and you can write a whole book of these
things. And you must have an office, and you use telephones, and you must have email. There’s not really
anything profound about that. So the question I like to ask in all of these situations in life where failure is the

When you look at the environment that we and those that came before us evolved in, and you just think about

We had predators running around stalking us that would literally attack someone right next to us and eat them.
That was part of reality. We also evolved in an environment where most of our time was spent trying to find

So there are two things that are important to remember about this, that humans are wired for instant

Notice when you get an emotional impulse or a physical impulse to do something that a picture comes up in

It’s even more extreme than this because we ingenious human beings have gone beyond instant gratification

so we can borrow some money from someone else and consume, and say, oh, you know what, I’ll worry about
that later. Unfortunately, things like credits cards and potato chips, they play to this wiring that’s so hard wired

for long-term thinking and for accumulation, and for the delaying of gratification. When we were running
around in the African Savannah, if you came across a little berry patch with ripe berries on it, hey, this wasn’t
the supermarket. There wasn’t a refrigerator around it, so you could just come back anytime you wanted and
get them. They were all consumed right now because there may never be another berry. Who knows?
When you’re managing a business, when you’re managing your time, managing people, managing resources,

to another person, one short-term decision, one little misstep can screw up a whole bunch of work. Warren
Buffett says it takes 20 years to build a reputation and five minutes to ruin it.

paradox. So if three of us are working on a project, and the project doesn’t go well, how am I a hundred percent
responsible and they’re a hundred percent responsible? Can’t we have like 33 percent each or something so that
if it fails they can be the ones that screwed it up, and if it succeeds, I did about 60 percent of the work? No. The
reality is in your world from where you stand in your life you chose it all at some level. You chose it all.
What I’ve discovered after many years of thinking about this and working on it and trying to like unpack this
puzzle, the conditions that you set up in life determine your results. The conditions that you set up in your life
determine your results. Not really the actions and communications and all that. That’s part of it. But when
those things are in service of setting up conditions, that’s when big results happen. So the conditions that you
have set up in your life are what determine your results. We’ll talk more about this later.
But guess what the most important condition that you have set up in your life that is determining results is?
Anyone? Conditions you’ve set up in your life. The people that you surround yourself with. The people that are
around you. That is by far the most important condition. Interesting experiments they’ve done where they’ve
looked at a person’s life, and they’ve asked them about their friends, and they’ll say, “Okay, how much money
do the closest five people in your life, how much money do they make?” And they average out those five people
and that’s how much money you make, so the conditions in your life that you’ve set up.

Eben Pagan: In order to become a successful manager of your business, it’s important to learn to think on a
level that most people don’t think on. It’s learning to think on the level of context, of conditions, of structures
to put in place in life and in business that create the outcomes that you want to have happen anyway. There’s
a great book called, The Path of Least Resistance by Fritz. Read it. The Path of Least Resistance. And he tells
an interesting story.
If you’ve ever been to Boston and walked the streets of Boston, you notice something. What do you notice?
They’re totally jacked up. There’s something wrong with them. Just intuitively, they’re not right. What’s going
on? Well, what’s happening is that when they went to make streets in Boston, they paved over the cow paths,
and so the city architecture of Boston has been roughly designed by the mindset of the 17th Century cow, which
is a little weird. But that’s reality. If it’s good enough for the cows, it’s good enough for streets.
Now, here’s an interesting point though. Why was it actually a good idea to pave over them? Because the
cows already found the path of least resistance. They already found the easiest way to maneuver around the
hills and so forth. So there’s actually some logic to it. But the problem is that most of us pave over cow paths
unconsciously, and when you pave over a cow path unconsciously – thank you guys for buying some children
some water, we really appreciate it that guys. Sneaky suckers. All right.
There’s a process that happens in the brain. It’s called myelination. When you think a particular thought, it
fires off your neurons in a certain pattern, and when that happens, this substance called myelin actually does a
process that’s not unlike paving over a path. And it kind of lays a road through that path, and it makes it easier
to think that thought next time. And when you think it again, more myelin happens, and more is laid on that
neurological pathway. And eventually, it becomes so myelinated that like a paved on road it’s just where you go
and drive with your thinking. That’s the way it happens.
So just like we pave cow paths out here, it happens neurologically inside of our brains when your thoughts
follow the path of least resistance. Now, when your thoughts follow the path of least resistance, what are they
usually following? Instant gratification thinking. That’ what they’re doing. They’re following how do I get my
needs met now? How do I get what I want? So you’re paving over counterproductive thinking. And this thing
happens without any conscious knowledge. You don’t have to think about doing it. It just happens. You got
it?
And eventually what happens when you pave over a cow path enough, or you myelinate your neurological
pathways enough, it becomes the difference between the Mississippi and the Colorado Rivers. They have the
Colorado River sneaking through the Grand Canyon. Have you ever flown over it in a plane and looked down?
Have you ever seen the Grand Canyon from a plane or seen one of those pictures from space? You realize that
this is a very significant little gash in the earth, and it just happened from water flowing through that channel
for millions and millions and millions of years.
And that’s what you get, and eventually the structures become so powerful that they’re almost impossible
to escape. Whereas a little stream that’s roughly on the surface, you can rechannel that. It’s very difficult to
change the path of the Colorado River in the Grand Canyon. That would be huge work beyond what you’d want
to take on. So it’s important to get at it while it’s upstream to do this stuff consciously so that the grooves that
get warn, and the patterns that get put in place and the myelination process that happens in your brain sets you
up for success and not for failure.
Remember this: people will follow the path of least resistance. As a general rule, human beings will follow
the path of least resistance. They’ll follow it mentally, they’ll follow it emotionally, and they will follow it
physically, and they’ll follow it in relationships, they’ll follow it as customers, they’ll follow it as employees,

They will come up with all kinds of rationalizations for why it made sense and why it’s good. They will walk

All the details, so planning the ritual is key. Having the details of exactly how it’s going to work. Now I like

be going through your normal life, doing your normal thing, in your normal groove, unconscious, and then
you’re gonna go do this new thing. Okay, you’re gonna be driving on the freeway one way and then you’re like

all that time in your workout because you’re not used to it. You forgot, oh, I was doing this extra thing and I
forgot to do it. And then the same thing with the off ramp, remerging back into your life.

Eben Pagan: Now we’re going to take the focus off of ourselves – I know, bummer – we’re going to move
into the area of business management called ‘People’. Now we’ve already talked about the high leverage that
you get from surrounding yourself with the type of person that you want to become. In business, selection is
critical; selection of the people that you partner with, selection of your customers, selection of the people that
come to work with your company. Jim Collins, who wrote Good to Great, said, “It’s the leader’s job to get the
right people on the bus and the wrong people off the bus.” Critical: get the right people on the bus and the
wrong people off the bus.
Brad Smart, who wrote the book Top Grading – has anyone heard me talk about Top Grading before? So
Brad Smart, who wrote Top Grading, says that the average mishire costs something like 20 times their annual
salary. That’s what it actually winds up costing when it’s all said and done and that 75 percent of people are
mishires. Seventy-five percent don’t work out. And he says, “Top grade; A players only.” Bill Gates has a great
quote. He says, “If you take away our top 20 people, Microsoft would become an unimportant company.”
That’s Bill Gates saying that; someone who probably would know. So what does this all point to?
It points to before you can manage people, you actually have to manage the process of bringing people
to work with you, whether they are contractors, whether they are employees, however you set that up
technically. Now what are the conditions you can set up to make sure that you only hire superstars; that you
only bring superstars to work with your business? How do you make sure that happens? Yell it out if you’ve
got an idea. Screen them, pay them well, criteria. Okay, criteria sounds to me more like conditions, good.
Incentivize them before you bring them on. How do you make sure that you bring on superstars? Critical
networking.
Swine flu. Be a superstar, good. That’s a good one. Reference check or the TORC technique; the threat of
reference check. I asked Brad Smart about the – he calls it the TORC techniques. The technique: Threat
of Reference Check. T-O-R-C, right the TORC technique and I said, “Could you make it sound a little more
friendly and maybe call it the PORC technique; the Promise of Reference Check? And he said, “Yeah, we call
it TORC, PORC, whatever.” The idea here is how do you put conditions in place so that only superstars and
only A players work with you? That’s the question to ask. Now, why do we want superstars? Why do we want
A players? Why is it so critical?
It’s critical because if you don’t get a superstar; if you get a C player or B player even, but if you get a C player,
they’re going to screw things up in so many ways that you can’t imagine. And that the short term pain relief
that you’re going to get by bringing one on to just do the things that you need them to do, is not work what
happens afterwards. So I said 75 percent of hires are mishires; this essentially means in a way that 75 percent
of people hired are a C player for people for that role. So they’re a C player for that role. And it’s actually even
more than that because a lot of people that keep their jobs long term are C players.
And what is a C player and how do they work? And how do you contrast that with an A player or a superstar?
Well, C players tend to have this thing that we’ve talked about that we call the ‘victim mentality’. They’re
at affect in the world; everything is happening to them. They try to distance themselves from problems
and failures and they try to connect themselves with successes. And you’ll hear them do that when they
talk. They also tend to be unusually driven by fear. So they’re not just focusing on problems like the average
person does; like looking at the obstacles and looking at the challenges; but they’re unusually motivated by
fear. They also, combined with these other special qualities of the victim mentality motivated by fear; they
also have an entitlement mentality they bring to the equation.
C players have more of that entitlement in their voice and an entitlement in their thinking than most people.
“I’m entitled; I showed up and I am entitled to a paycheck.” I watch a lot of documentaries and you watch a

because –.” And that’s really where he’s at; that’s really the place where he’s coming from in his life. Now I
can say, “You’re wrong and you’re a bad person.” And even the way I’m characterizing him right now sounds
like I’m being critical, but I’m just trying to say that that’s where he’s at as a business person.
Most people in most jobs are in a place like that where, “My thing, I’m entitled to show up and do this thing
because I’ve made it make sense in my mind and you’re robbing, you’re hurting me if you don’t keep giving
me what you were giving me before.” And that can be a real problem if you hire someone to work for you
in your business because C players also tend to be the ones that like to sue you when things don’t work out
because they don’t have other options. A players and superstars are in demand. They’ve always got other
options, they can always go do something else and they don’t want to waste their time getting into some
conflict – “I’ll just get on with my life.”
C players have got nothing to lose; they are the ones that are the filers of the lawsuits in my experience and
in the experience of others that I know. So you have another incentive that’s a little more fear based to
avoid C players. They also tend, in my experience, to have a low interest in business. They’re just not that
interested in becoming experts at business, raising themselves up as experts. They tend to have low intrinsic
motivation; they have low aspirations; they tend to have low esteem for themselves, for others, for the
business itself. And they’re in the paradigm of job and they say things like, “I need to get a job to make some
money.”
They don’t say, “I’m going to challenge myself with a new experience so that I can learn something so I can
develop my career.” That’s the way A players talk and superstars; that’s where they’re coming from. C players
say, “I need to get a job so I can make some money.” Okay, so low aspirations, low motivation, low interest in
business. Now there are a couple of things that C players are spectacular at; where a C player shines and they
become superstars. One of them is building what I call, ‘black boxes’: creating systems inside of a business
that only they know how to operate. Fun.
And the other thing that they’re spectacular at, which black boxes are a part of, is creating job security for
themselves; wiring themselves into the business in such a way where they’re indispensible; holding some
knowledge so that if you ever let go of them, you’re screwed. So they’re great at creating job security and
building black boxes. Most people that are hired by most business at all levels are C players. They are C
players. If you’ve heard of the Peter Principle – anyone here know what the Peter Principle is? The Peter
Principle is that we rise to our level of incompetence in business and that the reason why when you interact
with business people in corporations, they just seem to suck, no matter who you’re talking to and at what
level, is because you do well at one role and you get a promotion, and the promotion is to a different set of
responsibilities; you have a different role.
You do well there and you go to the next level and each time there’s a transformation; there’s a new set of
things that you have to learn how to do; a new level of thinking. And at some point you rise up to a level

70

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 5
where you’ve gone beyond your competency or you’ve moved into an area that’s not your strength and so you
stop there and you can’t go any further because you’re not a high performer there. And that at some level in
business, this is what’s happened in the corporate world is everyone has risen up to the level where they’re
now incompetent at that thing and they can’t go any further. And God forbid that anyone would go back to
something that they’re good at because they wouldn’t want to take the status hit. You follow?
So even people that were A players at what they were doing, in a lot of cases they’re working in roles where
they’re not or they can’t be because of the way it’s set up. And that’s what we’re dealing with; that’s the reality.
If you bring C players into your business, you are putting a boat anchor around your neck while you’re trying
to swim. You’re tying a big ball and chain to your leg while trying to run a race. You’re handicapping yourself
beyond almost anything that you could do because they will come into your business, they will build black
boxes, they will create job security, they’ll make friends with the other C players and probably most important
of all, a C player is A player kryptonite.
They’re like taking a skunk and getting it in your car and shaking it up until it sprays its scent inside your car
and then calling up your friends and saying, “Hey, you want to go for a drive?” They’re repellant for A players.
Superstars don’t want to work around C players. If you’ve got three A players and you bring in a C player,
the C player will turn the three A players into B players; pull them down. And it also says that you tolerate
that kind of thing in business. Now why do we hire C players? Why do we bring them on in the first place?
Desperation, they’re cheap. Why else? They’re available. How about this? They’re related to us. They need
a job; they’re a friend; I really want to help them out.
I think that a lot of C players are hired with generosity in mind. I actually think that there’s a charitable
approach when a lot of C players are hired. “You know what? We’ll just put them over here; they can’t
really screw anything up too bad.” I have watched people that I love and care about get bitten in the ass and
essentially screwed over by C players; threatened, have their businesses screwed up, sued and it’s a bummer.
It’s really harsh to watch someone who did someone a favor; like really was trying to do good; really had the
intention to do good and isn’t trying to hurt the other person get screwed over it. This is the reality.
If you want to avoid that stuff, you have to make a commitment to yourself: No more C players. No more C
players in my life. We hired Brad Smart a few years ago to do some consulting for our business and to train
our managers and after he taught us and we did some exercises for a couple of days, I had an aha! I had an
epiphany which was that in my life, when I experience frustration, when I experience I’m frustrated with a
business situation, it’s almost always because of one of two reasons: 1.) Because the person I’m interacting
with is not an A player and 2.) I need to get something done and I don’t have an A player that I can interact
with to get the job done.
That was pretty big for me to realize that. Wow, the things that are challenging and frustrating in business:
A player, lack of A player, non-A player issues. Big mistakes that people make when hiring, when partnering,
when outsourcing – I think these are the two big ones: 1.) Hiring people that you like and 2.) Hiring people
that are like you and they’re usually the same person. Hiring people that you like and hiring people that are
like you. You probably don’t need more of you in your business. You probably need more people that aren’t
like you. First, we have swine flu and then earthquake and three glasses broken. Let’s just call it a day guys.
Here’s another rule of thumb that I discovered: smooth talkers usually are not smooth doers in business.
Meaning that they’re inconsistent; they’re all over the map. If they’re super charming and you’re like, “Wow,
this person just talks a great game,” that’s when you need to get nervous. That’s the person to watch out for.
Now if you’re hiring them to do a sales role or presentations and that’s what their role is, great. But if it’s
something that requires consistency and discipline, someone that’s a super smooth talker, often times will

71

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 5
be a C player in that role. And people can be a C player in one role and an A player in a different one, but
generally speaking, A players are A players and C players are C players. Now let’s contrast – by the way, who
can make the commitment, no more C players in your life?
No more C players. When you surround yourself with C players, you become one yourself; there’s no win.
Now what are the qualities that an A player or a star or a superstar has? And why is it so important to do
what it takes to get these? And I also mention, superstar A players are not looking through the classified ads
on Craig’s List saying, “Boy, I’ve got to find my next gig or I’m not going to be able to make rent.” That’s not
usually where they’re at. You can usually find them on Craig’s List and we have hired superstars on Craig’s
List and on Monster.com and what have you. You have to sift through a lot more people in order to find them.
But where are superstars and A players? They’re working; they’re typically happily working in some cool
company doing something neat and you have to have something cooler; a better opportunity. And you
usually have to get to know them longer – that swine fly is spreading guys; be careful. Does anyone have one
of those masks on you? You got one? Bring it down here; I want to wear it. You got one right on you there?
Okay cool. I’m going to throw that on here in a minute if you guys keep sneezing at me. There’s nothing to
sneeze at. So to cultivate relationships with A players takes longer. The process of building a relationship of
attracting them takes longer; it takes more work.
But it’s like getting married. People that meet in Vegas and run down to the chapel and get married – I’ll bet
of we looked at the statistics, overall, those relationships maybe didn’t work out as happy in the long run as
people that got to know each other a little better. So I’m going to talk about a few qualities that we look for in
our company when we’re trying to find stars; we call them a star. And the first quality – and this one actually
has several sub-qualities and I’ll share a few of those – that’s the most important, is called being a driver. And
a driver is someone who is proactive. They show up and they say, “Give me stuff to do. What else needs to be
done?”
They also are result oriented. They have a conceptualization of what we’re trying to achieve and they don’t
just say, “Give me a job.” They say, “I want to own something. I want to own a result.” When you get that
magical combination of someone who understands the result, is proactive and has a sense of ownership, it’s
very very powerful. And they’re also persistent. A contrast that I like to make is if a C player comes to work
at your company and they’re working on a project and they send you and email and they say, “Hey, I have
a question for you.” And then they don’t hear back from you, the C player will just kind of fold up and not
make any trouble and not say anything.
A few weeks later you’ll say, “Hey where’s that project at?” And they’ll go, “Well, I emailed you and I –.”
Whereas an A player, a star, if they don’t hear back from you, they pick up the phone and call and if you don’t
answer the phone, they’re knocking on the door. It’s not personal, it’s not weird. They’re just there because
they’re trying to get the thing done; they’re trying to get the job done; they’re trying to achieve whatever it
is they’re trying to achieve. They’re trying to move up in the business and they’re trying to make something
happen. And they say, “Hey, I emailed you; I called you. I just need to get this from you quick.” Have you
ever heard, ‘if you need something done, give it to a busy person’?
They’re talking about stars there; they’re talking about superstars. You’ll also find that stars typically have
aspirations. They have personal and professional aspirations and when you say to a star, “Where would you
like to be in three years?” they’ll have an answer. They’ll know where they’re going. They’ll say, “Well, I’d
like to be at this level and have this type of role. I’d like to make this much money and I’d like to know these
skills.” Or they’ll say personally, “Well, I want to buy a house in three years.” They aspire to things that are
bigger than where they are at right now. And it’s not, “I want a job.” They see a bigger future for themselves.

72

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 5
They see a bigger them in the future. They also have, what I call, ‘millionaire next door values’ or ‘millionaire
next door sensibilities’.
Thomas Stanley wrote the book The Millionaire Next Door and he went out and he interviewed millionaires
and what he found is that the average millionaire isn’t driving a big fancy Rolls Royce and flying around
on a private jet and drinking expensive champagne. The average millionaire lives in an old established
neighborhood and drives a ten-year-old used care and dresses in comfortable non-flashy clothes, isn’t trendy
and is four times as likely to carry a Sears card because they’re practical. I was talking to Homer McDonald
who wrote the book, Stop Your Divorce – I was doing an interview with him and we were actually talking
about The Millionaire Next Door in another context and he said, “Why is it that millionaires live in older
houses and drive used cars and don’t dress flashy and they don’t –”
Because they have self-esteem. They’re not seeking other people’s approval. They’re not trying to make
the money to be flashy. They’re not trying to get other people to like them; they like themselves. They like
what they’re doing with their life and they do what makes sense to them. That’s what you’re looking for in a
superstar. You’ll often see a – if you meet a star; if they come to an interview with you, they might be dressed
professionally, but in a lot of cases, maybe they’ll be wearing slacks and a nice shirt, but maybe they’ll have
their sleeves rolled up and maybe they’ll have them pushed up a little bit.
So they’re business presentable, but they’re not trying to have the extra flashy little details to show off. A
superstar that you’re going to hire to do something to do work with you will rarely roll up their sleeves and be
wearing some glittery watch or some jewels where they’re really trying to show off. Because that’s not what
life is about for them. Life is about growth and development and achieving things for themselves, internally
and externally; mastering. You following me here? So in a way, superstars are kind of camouflaged. They’re
not standing on the street corner with a sign that says, “Will work for food.” They’re not the super flashy
types.
And they’re not looking for a job in the paper. They’re rare and if you want them, you must find them. You
must go out and seek them out. The way to seek them out, by the way, is to spend some amount of time
everyday or every week – recommend a little bit of time everyday – building relationships with stars. On
average, we know something like 150 or 200 people each. That’s the average number of people that comes
to a wedding, funeral; it’s kind of the tribal structure size. Human tribes evolve together. Who here know
probably a couple hundred people at that level that I’m talking about? Raise your hand. Now if I asked you,
“Who are the superstars of your 200 person group?”
And I’m not talking about your 5,000 Facebook friends or the 17,000 that follow you on twitter that you don’t
really know. I’m talking about the people you actually know. Who could start thinking and realize, “You
know, of the 200, probably ten or 20 of them are really the stars; really the rock stars of life.” Who could you
start thinking of those people right now? Those are the people that you want to start hanging out with more;
you want to spend more time around. And you want to get them to introduce you to other people that they
know because people travel with other people like them. They’re a rock star; they probably have a few others
around them that are like them and you want to meet those people.
And as you’re building what Brad Smart calls your ‘personal bench’, you want to be calling those people up
and saying, “You know, I’m going to need someone to help me out with my technology in probably a year
if my business keeps on this trajectory and I’d love to start a relationship with someone now. Do you know
anyone who’s a good HTML website builder? Because that’s what I’m going to need.” And you just ask
those people and they’ll introduce you to someone and if they don’t know someone, you ask the next magic
question, which is, “Who do you know that would know that person?”

And you network. I actually think – this is one of those things that I’m – I can’t prove this and who knows
what the future will hold, but I’m going to really put it out there right now – but, everybody’s trying to figure
out in this moment how to make a lot of money using social networking and social media; like, “How do I
turn Twitter into money? How do I turn Facebook into money? How do I turn LinkedIn into money? How
do I sell stuff to those people?” And I don’t really think that that’s what’s going to happen. I think that what
everybody’s missing, in a large way – I mean a lot of people will figure out how to make money from social
networking, sure, but the real value of it is the social networking.
That’s where all the value is, is that you can actually go and connect with and meet and learn from – you
follow? So trying to figure out how to use the tool, which is a spectacular tool – if you get a good network
on LinkedIn or you’ve got a bunch of followers on Twitter and you need something done; you need to find
somebody who knows a lot about something and you need to start connecting to stars, you can do it so fast
now with access to so many different people and you can do it through a trust network quickly, facilitated
by technology – awesome! I think that most people are trying to figure out, “How do I use this thing, which
actually gives me something way better than money, to make money.”
It’s like having the telescope the wrong way and being like, “Why does everything look so small?” So build
your virtual bench, build the relationships, dig your well before you’re thirsty, meet and get to know and
network with and get input from and help stars. Just get used to doing that and do it all the time. I spend a
lot of my time; I don’t spend, I invest a lot of my time helping out up and coming superstars, many of which
I will never work with. I’ll never actually work with them in some way. Some I will and that will be very
rewarding; that will be great, but many I will not. Why would I do that?
Because when I need to meet people, those people are going to open up a door that I could have never gotten
into because I’ve contributed to them and they’re stars and I know you’re an up and coming rock star. Five to
tem years from now you’re going to know like 50 people that I’m going to want to know; so I’m going to help
you now and everybody wins. We all win. So build your virtual bench for your business, but also build your
virtual personal virtual bench of your network and use social networking for what it’s good for. When you
get connected to someone that’s really cool, don’t say, “Hey, can you give me some attention and give me some
free stuff? What can you do for me?”
Help them; do something cool for them and support them in some way. A month or so ago I was on some trip
and it turned out Brad Smart, who’s the author of Top Grading, was actually also on the trip because we have
a mutual friend in common. So I got to hang out with him a lot and talk to him. And he’s really focusing
top grading right now; he’s really stripping it down to the highest leverage pieces. And he was talking about
the one quality that it all comes down to; that he looks for when he’s interviewing people. And the quality is
resourcefulness. That’s what it comes down to is resourcefulness. C players get stopped, “I don’t know how to
do this.
We got into this trouble and we couldn’t get out of it. A problem came up and I’m blaming that.” Stars, the
A players, they’re resourceful. “We didn’t have enough resources, but we figured out how to make it happen
anyway; we got the job done. We snatched success from the jaws of defeat; snatched victory from the jaws
of defeat.” Resourcefulness. A couple other quick little tips. I got to ask Steve Ballmer, “What do you look
for when you’re hiring people?” He’s the CEO of Microsoft; billionaire. He said, “Two things: passion and
track record successfully driving projects to completion.” What do those things add up to? Driver, A player,
resourceful; these all overlap.
Brad Smart recommends a process called The Serial In-Depth Interview where you interview someone and
you actually get them to go through every job and every position and every role they’ve had and if you can

get them all the way back to school, do that. And go through the whole thing and ask a series of questions
and look for the patterns to see whether or not they’re resourceful or not. And they won’t know – they won’t
be able to – they won’t be conceptualizing along the level of you’re looking for patterns; they’re just going to
be answering. And what happens is the C player says, “Well, I took the job and I thought it was going to be
a good job, but it turns out I had a bad boss and then this went wrong and that went wrong.” “Why did you
leave?”
“Oh, well, I was downsized.” “And then what happened?” “Well, I went and found another job over here.”
And you can here the pattern coming out. Now I’ll tell you something right now. I have never met someone
in business yet – I’m sure it will happen – but I’ve never met someone who has said, “We waste too much time
interviewing and getting to know potential candidates who come to work for us.” I’ve never heard someone
say that and I talk to a lot of people. I’ve never heard somebody say, “We waste too much time interviewing
people and just getting to know them and figuring out who they are as potential candidates.”
On the other hand, I have heard probably from every single person that’s ever hired people to work for them,
“I’ve heard too fast. I made the mistake of hiring too fast based on emotion, not getting to know them long
enough.” Does that make sense? Is there anyone here who has spent just too much time getting to know
people and interviewing them over their history of their business and career? Is there anyone here who has
hired someone too fast in your life? So what does that mean? What can we learn from that? Way better
to get to know people in depth, to discipline yourself, to do what it takes to get to know them over time. So
here’s what we’re going to do.
We’re going to do a little sample of this. And what I want you to do is write down a series of questions. This is
a miniature version of a top grading interview that you’re about to do. It’s a miniature version of top grading.
I’m going to give you a set of questions to ask, you’re going to get a partner and you’re going to ask them this
series of questions. You’re going to start with the first job that they ever had. And if you can go back further;
like maybe they had a student counsel position at their high school or maybe they had a part-time job at a
lemonade stand when they were 13 or whatever; the earliest position that they can remember having. And
you’re going to say: What were your expectations going into the job? What did you expect?
And then you’re going to ask: What were the projects that you worked on? What were your successes? What
were your failures? And why did you leave? So you’re going to ask: What were your expectations going in?
What were the projects that you worked on? And you want all of them; one after another. What were your
successes? What were your failures? And why did you leave? Does everybody have those? Do you want
me to say them again? No? What were your expectations going in? Projects you worked on? What were
your successes? What were your failures? Why did you leave? And then I want you to ask the all-important
question: when I talk to your old boss, what are they going to say were your strengths and weaknesses?
When I talk to your boss from that job, what are they going to say were your strengths and weaknesses? And
then, what was your next job? If you really want to get savvy, you can figure out how much space was between
them because superstars, A players, usually don’t have space between their jobs, whereas C players often have
big gaps and they conceal them. Brad Smart says, “Resumes are almost worthless.” Looking at a person’s
resume is almost worthless. Read Top Grading if you want a better system to use than resume. Top Grading
by Brad Smart. So you understand the five questions and then the one bonus question? What’s your boss
going to say when I talk to them? What are your strengths and weaknesses?
Now this is something, by the way, that if you do a good job at it, with kind of an entry-level person, will
take a couple of hours. If you’re interviewing a senior person, it could take four or five hours to go through
the whole thing. So you’re doing a very abbreviated version right now. Do you understand? So what you’re

person out.
So as we move more into a knowledge economy, I recommend that you start doing this on a regular basis. If

And a lot of good will come of it and I really think that this is where we’re going in the future; where we’re

Say to them, say, “Are you interested in learning about Twitter?” And if they say, “Yeah, I’d love to learn

tell you everything I know about Twitter and you tell me everything you know about exercise.” Every area of

Now in business, when you’re hiring somebody to come to work with you or you’re outsourcing a project to

When you’re dealing with the minutia of business and administrative problems and all that stuff, you forget

So let’s drill down into the concept of trust. Very little work, very little research, very little thought

look at the trust books bookshelf and then you look at the HTML coding bookshelf, the HTML section is like

multiple shelves of books and the trust shelf: 1.) There isn’t one and 2.) There are like three good books on
the topic and most of them came out in the last five years. There’s just not much about trust. Most people

Steven Covey, who wrote The Seven Habits of Highly Effective People, his son, Steven Covey, wrote a book
called The Speed of Trust and it’s a really interesting concept. I’ve been looking at and studying trust for
probably 13 or 14 years when I saw this – there’s nothing about it and even the books that were available when
I first started looking at it were very kind of obscure and strange. Now we’re just starting to realize, “Oh, trust
is really important.” And here’s what The Speed of Trust suggests and I love how he – great name, The Speed
of Trust. Trust speeds up business. Trust makes business faster, cleaner and more efficient.
If two people are working together and they don’t trust each other, the communication isn’t going to go very
quickly because they’re going to suspect each other’s motives, they’re not going to be on the same page, and
they’re not going to believe that they have each other’s best interest in mind. Whereas when two people trust
each other, the speed increases dramatically because the communication dials up to a broadband level. So
lack of trust slows down business; increase of trust speeds up business. So how do you increase trust? What
do you do to increase trust? Human almost by definition – human equals more than one. So humans don’t
exist in isolation.
We didn’t evolve in isolation; we evolved in groups. We evolved in family sized clans and groups of family
clans that made up tribes. So the definition of human kind of includes there’s more than one of us in the
picture. Now we also have this fascinating other side of the coin which is that we like to be individuals,
especially in this modern time, and we like to think of ourselves as I’m an individual and I’m independent and
it’s very valuable to go through an independent phase in life and to see yourself as independent to really take
responsibility for your life.
When you wrap around this that there’s some part of us that’s wired to sync with other people; these mirror
neurons – what Daniel Goldman, who writes about social intelligence; how our brains are wired to sync and
how we just automatically synchronize and imitate what other people are doing around us and the power
of the reference group and the mastermind. So there’s something going on in our old circuitry and our old
wiring that makes us a group animal and makes us a neuron in the group brain. If you – don’t make me do
that. If you take a mouse and you put it in a little cage that has electricity running through the floor and you
shock it through its feet and it gets very afraid and starts freaking out – and by the way I love – you know this
right?
I love scientists who torture animals and then write books about them. Brutal, but we learn interesting
stuff from it. Then you take the mouse out in pain and fear and freaking out and you put it in a cage with
other mice, they will smell the fear and they will go into fear. The pheromones, the chemical signals will
unconsciously affect the other mice. So we affect each other on an unconscious level all the time. I think
that just like a dog knows if you like it – if a dog comes running into the room, and it’s like checking you out
and it doesn’t know you, it can tell do you like dogs. And if you don’t like dogs and you’re like, “Get that dog
away from me,” the dog will realize you don’t like it or maybe you don’t like dogs and then it’s not going to
like you. [Break in Audio 1:19:21.5 to 1:19:26.6]. On the other hand, if the dog comes running into the room
and it’s wagging its tail and you’re happy to see the dog and you start petting it, the dog will know that you
like the dog. Well people know if you like them or not. It doesn’t matter what you do, we know – like I know
if you like me. My friend Murray says something interesting, he says, “Trust is a virtue.” And some people
say, “How can it be a virtue?” Hang on a second; I need to get a drink of water because I’m getting the frog
in the throat effect. The swine flu stuff is not funny. Actually, to me it is kind of funny, but it’s another topic
entirely.

What I love is that they’re like, “Seventeen people died of swine flu over here.” “Yeah, but during the same
period, like a thousand people died of the regular flu over here. Calm down.” Settle down, Beavis. What was
I talking about before I got the swine flu? Virtue, right. Thank you. I didn’t know that it messed up your
memory. Weird. So how can trust be a virtue? It’s not like patience; that’s more like an inner quality that you
cultivate. The reason that trust is a virtue is because it’s something that you do first. It’s to trust. So if you
want someone to trust you, trust them. Trust them first. If you extend trust to them, then their mechanism
will come on and go, “Oh, someone trusts me. I can extend trust back.”
Whereas if you do the normal human thing, which is to treat someone that you’ve hired and that you’re
working with kind of like this robot or machine and I’m supposed to keep this distance, give you money and
you’re supposed to give me this service, you’re not going to cultivate this trust. So when managing people, it’s
very important: trust them. Extend them more trust than you think they deserve and when they violate your
trust, give them some more trust and keep working on it. And you know what, if it doesn’t work out, it turns
out that they’re not right, let them go, but making yourself trust people and extend it to them will allow them
to rise up and also trust you more and then you can communicate faster.
It’s also important to always watch the back of the other person that you’re working with. Watch their back.
Keep your eye out. Make sure that they know that they’re not going to take a fall because of what you did;
that you’re going to take the fall. If something comes up and it’s one of those political situations where
it’s just not going to look good, look at them and say, “You know what? I’ll take the fall on that one. I was
involved; I’ll take that one.” And just do it. Not with the ‘you’re going to owe me one’ kind of thing in mind,
but just say, “You know what? I got this one.” Take responsibility because the reality is when you stand up
and you take responsibility and you say to whomever, you say, “You know what? It was my bad. I made the
mistake. I’m responsible for it. I’ll fix it.”
Everyone is going to respect you more anyway even the person you made the mistake with. And it just
creates a great role model for everyone. You know they say the captain should go down with the ship. My
friend, who was on the plane that crashed, said the captain was the last one off. Last one off the plane. Very
interesting. Do you remember how he was hailed as a hero? I could imagine that if he was pushing out
the window and jumping out the front of the plane and holding on to his little seat cushion out there, that
it wouldn’t have quite been like that. The story wouldn’t have been that way. If your team is working late
nights, you work late nights.
If they’re pulling hard, you pull hard. A friend of mine was in the Israeli military and the Israeli military
has a policy that the most senior ranking officer is always at the front of any charge. So if there’s a battalion
of tanks charging the enemy, the most senior ranking officer is in the front tank going into battle. Why?
Because nobody that ranks below that officer is going to question whether or not their hearts in it. Now they
also lose a lot of senior officers, it turns out, because they’re at the front. But that’s the way to make trust
happen; that’s the way to create camaraderie and the group spirit, is you go first. You charge in there first; you
make it happen.
Also, just as a little tip here, make sure you’re congruent. Make sure you’re not walking in to tell your team,
“We can’t afford to have Styrofoam cups in the coffee room anymore. Look at my new Rolex.” That kind of
stuff doesn’t go over well; that screws up trust. So remember your business and your personal life when you
become the boss, you lose the ‘I can complain, I can bitch about what my life is like’. No, you’re the boss.
You’re on easy street. It doesn’t matter if you’re making any money or not or whether the business is working
or not, that’s your role. You’ve got the privilege of doing that, so that’s what you’ve got to be.
So focus on trust. It’s the key to speed. Trust is the key to speed in managing people. Shoot straight with

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 6
people. Be straight with them; be candid. Cultivate the ability to let all your stuff from your childhood and
all your issues with people and all your issues about money and let it go. In business, you cannot afford to
beat around the bush, be politically correct, waste time, and not tell someone what’s going on because you’re
trying to save their feelings. It’s not what anyone wants. Jack Welch talks about how important it is to be
candid with people and to be straight with them. I used to always really have a problem with this. I’d always
be like, “How do I figure out how to say this so that it won’t upset them or it won’t hurt their feelings or it
won’t –.”
What I’ve learned is that that’s really not what people want. Most of the time people get upset at other people
not because they were straight with them, but because they were playing games with them. I used to – with
friends of mine that were particularly emotional people; emotionally sensitive – we all know people that are
highly emotionally sensitive, like a leaf falls off a tree and they’re like, “It’s dying!” Some people are just more
emotionally sensitive. I used to be really hesitant to tell them where it was at or to be like open and honest
with them because I didn’t want to hurt their feelings; I never wanted to upset them so I’d be like, “You know,
well, like, you know –.”
What I learned is that those types of people appreciate directness and candor more than other people. You
know why? Because everybody does that with them and they don’t like it and they don’t know why. Because
from their perspective, they’re not getting upset and they’re not emotionally sensitive because of feedback
and people being straight with them. They’re emotionally sensitive because they’re just more sensitive to
everything that’s going on around them. You follow? And when you sit down with someone that’s highly
emotionally sensitive and you say, “I want to give you some straight feedback about your performance.
I’ve noticed that the results that you’re getting are inconsistent and I don’t think that that’s going to get you
where you want to go long term and I kind of think that you need to have a little wake up call here and I care
about you and I want you to get this.” They will usually say, “Thank you so much for telling me this. I really
appreciate you coming forward.” And it usually creates a real space. So candor has to be done on faith. You
have to be straight with people; you have to stand in the fire knowing that they might get upset and they
might freak out and sometimes they do, but generally speaking, it is by far the best policy.
And you notice, too, that when leaders get into hot what and it’s like, “Did you sleep with that woman?” They
go, “I did not sleep with that woman.” “Did you sleep with that woman? You know you’re kind of a hoser if
you did, but you’re human and –” It’s when they say, “I did not sleep with that woman. You know, read my
lips. I didn’t do it.” And it comes out like, “Not only did you sleep with her, but you did a lot of other really
kind of weird stuff, too.” And you’re like, “Oh, what was I doing?” But that’s what we do, right? The ego
comes up and we want to preserve this whole persona and image that we think other people have of us. That’s
all a waste for the most part; preserving the image that you think people have of you.
Just be yourself and be straight with people. When you try to save another person’s feelings by not being
honest with them, you actually and in almost all cases, wind up hurting their feelings a lot worse down the
road. Sometimes you have to hurt a person’s feelings in order to help them get to the next level, especially
in business. But if you can get past your own stuff and you can sit there and you can be present for them and
you can say, “Here’s some feedback. It’s honest, it’s straight feedback, it’s how I see things and I care about
you and I’m telling you this and I’m standing in the weirdness here because I care about you,” they may have
an emotional rollercoaster; they may go up and down, but eventually they’ll say, “Thank you. Thank you very
much for being honest with me and straight with me. I really appreciate it.” Whereas if you don’t, if you beat
around the bush and you avoid it, there’s going to be long term weirdness that will be around and it will just
accumulate.

85

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 6
Also, don’t gossip, don’t play favorites, and don’t do any of that stuff in business. The chimp part of your
brain will want to do it and it will naturally do it. Don’t talk about people behind their back; avoid all that
stuff. One of the things that I do – and really I learned this from Steven Covey is – you know, he said he went
to work and – I was telling this story recently, but I can’t remember exactly what it was, but he went to work
somewhere; I think he went to a school, maybe, and he had a run in with a senior person there so he went to
the dean or the head of the organization and he said, “This person’s a total screw up. I’m mad at them and
you should fire them.”
And so the boss there or the dean or whoever it was said, “Hang on a minute,” and called that person into
the room and said, “Okay, say that again.” He was like, “Oh, wait a minute.” That’s the way to handle things,
I think, generally speaking. If you’ve got two people that are working with you and one of them is starting
to freak out, say “You know what, let’s get the other person in here, and let’s figure this out.” And do not
take sides, don’t defend anyone and work it out. Let everybody air their feelings and move on. As it turns
out, in my own study of human trust and relationship and bonding, conflict and hurt feelings and missed
expectations are the foundation for intimacy.
So think about that for a second. So figuring out, working through conflicts, sharing feelings over issues,
revealing what’s really going on inside of you, bonds and it actually creates more intimacy. Now are there
exceptions? Of course, there are exceptions, like everything. But generally speaking, if you think about the
people that you have the most deepest, most intimate relationships with, those are the people who you’ve
gone through some of the biggest challenges. You follow? That’s where some of the bigger, emotional
conflicts have come and where you had to work through those things and where in the middle, you didn’t
know if it was going to work out or the relationship was going to live.
There’s a great phrase: sometimes you have to risk a relationship to have a relationship. So you’ve got to risk
the relationship; you’ve got to be willing to risk it and you’ve got to say, “I’m willing to risk this relationship
because I care about you and I want to go to the next level rather than having this be something that drives
between us.” So don’t play any of those games and don’t do anything that can risk your reputation. Just avoid
that stuff. Stay away from it; it’s not good for you. The next point when you’re shooting straight is: tell people
where it’s at, get as centered as you can possibly be, say what’s on your mind, try to not justify it or rationalize
it too much and then listen to the other person’s feelings until they feel heard.
This is a weird set of words: feel heard; but most people know what it means. So after you give someone
some feedback or some information or some coaching, you give them – especially if it’s critical and you do it
with good intention, they’re usually going to have something that’s going to come up. They’re going to feel
threatened or they might feel happy and thankful – whatever it is, usually there’s an emotional process. And
what a lot of people do is they say, “Okay, that’s all. Get out.” And the person has to go process. I find it
valuable to say, “How do you feel about what I just said,” if they haven’t already.
And then as they share their feelings, listen and you can even use the words, you can say – at the end of whole
thing, you can say, “Do you feel heard? Do you feel like I understand you?” And if they say, “Yes, I do feel
heard,” you’re in good shape. So listen to their feelings because even though their feelings are irrational and
emotional and all that stuff, they’re still relevant and they still matter. So listen until the person feels heard.
Now I’d like to teach you a techniques that I kind of think of as being the ubertechnique for all management
and leadership of people. It’s kind of a meta-model in a way that I use in marketing, in partnering, in
managing people.
There’s a specific application that we’ll talk about here. And this is alignment and specifically aligning
whatever it is that you’re trying to do with their future aspirations. So aligning with their aspirations and it

86

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 6
works like this – let me give you the theory of it and why it’s important. If you don’t know another person’s
aspirations, if you don’t know where they’re trying to go; where their model is, what their values are, if you
don’t understand where they’re at and where they’re trying to go, then you essentially can’t communicate with
them in a way that makes sense to them. You can’t communicate in a way that will motivate them.
They won’t want to go where you’re trying to get them to go and you won’t be able to get the results that you
want and you won’t be able to influence them to go the direction that they need to go even for their own
good. On the other hand, if you know what their model is and where they’re trying to go, it’s like having
the teacher’s edition of the math book with all the answers in it because now you understand how they’re
motivated and what they’re trying to do. So now, you have a lot of influence power and you can really get
them to go where you want them to go. And, by the way, they’ll feel a lot more trust for you and they will
appreciate you a lot more.
A person’s aspirations in their life – and again, we’re only working with A players and superstars here for the
moment, but this works with anyone – a person’s aspiration; what they’re trying to get or what they’re trying
to avoid in the future of their life is a lot more motivating long term and in the big picture than anything else
you’re going to come up with randomly. Now, yeah you can put a gun to their head and you can argue, “Well,
if I had a gun to their head, that could be motivating to them,” but I don’t think that we live in that kind of an
environment anymore. I think we live in an environment where we want to be able to keep people engaged
and motivated and interested long term and the way to do that is to understand where they’re going.
The ironic weird reality about us humans is that we don’t like to ask other people where they’re going and
then listen to what they say and get the words and clarify and make sure that we really get it. What we like to
do is guess and then say, “No, I get it. I know exactly what you mean.” And if you don’t believe me, just think
about the way your parents used to talk about each other because my experience is that two people that are
together for a long time, they really start thinking that they really know where the other person is coming
from and what they’re trying to do and that this, in a lot of ways, is at the heart of a lot of the conflict.
If you say – if they have conflict; there are probably two of us in here that came from that perfect home where
nobody fought and even the dog was happy all the time and whatever. I’m mad at you whoever you are; I
just want you to know. But if you look in family situations and a person does something and you say to the
person, “Well, but do you understand where they’re coming from and what they’re trying to do?” “Yeah, I
know what they’re trying to do. They’re trying to piss me off.” “No, they weren’t.” “Yeah, they were.” And
once you understand; it’s these little distinctions. Once you understand that other model, you go, “Oh, I
didn’t even – oh, you had that other little perspective there that was affecting all this.
Now that I get it, it all makes sense to me.” So how do you figure out what where another person’s going and
what their aspiration is? Ask. That’s it. You ask and you don’t assume that you get it. And you keep asking
and clarifying and clarifying and asking and clarifying and saying it in different ways almost until the person
says, “Okay, you understand me better than anyone ever has; let’s move on from here. I feel a little too well
understood.” Who here feels a little too well understood by other people in their life? One person; two. You
feel too well understood? So out of a few hundred people, there are always two or three or four neurotic
people in the room who are borderline crazy; they don’t get it. You also came from the family that I hate, too,
right?
No, you didn’t come from that family. Okay, good. Well then, you’re not too bad. But most people, as you
can see, never feel understood enough. They really never feel understood enough. And for the few that
do, consider yourself very fortunate because it’s tough to not feel understood. So ask a person, “Where are
you going? What are you trying to accomplish?” And then once you have that, once you understand where

87

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 6
the person’s trying to go, what they’re trying to do and once you really get it and you have that, “Ah, we have
aligned,” that’s when it’s time to start using that to help them get there.
And so if you need to give them some critical feedback, it’s not, “You’re screwing up and this is a warning and
if you don’t correct your behavior, you’re out,” it’s “You know, you’re a superstar. I know that you are. You
perform great in this and this and this area and I assume that something must be going on right now and I
give you the benefit of the doubt. And I know that you’re trying to go here in three years in your life, I know
where you’re trying to go, and I see that the things that you’ve been doing lately are going in this direction
over here. And I care about you and I want to see you get where you want to go; I’m excited about your vision.
So I think you need to go this direction. This is the course you need to be on.”
When you say, “Stop – you’re late everyday and you’re screwing up and I’m mad at you and you’re in trouble
and blah, blah, blah and all this negative stuff,” what does the other person do? In their mind, they
rationalize, they justify and they make you into the bastard or the beotch, in more modern terminology,
and they reserve in the back of their head, they’re like, “The next time you screw up, I’m going to have this
to use as a weapon that you were mean to me.” And we store that stuff up; we remember it and that’s how
resentment works.
On the other hand, if you say to the person, “Hey, I care about you, I’m open, I know where you want to go,
I see the direction you’re going right now and yeah, I’ve got my own feelings about it, but you’re not going
the direction that you want to go and this situation isn’t working out to get you there,” what are they going
to do? Most people have never had someone who’s so on their side that they’re actually saying to them, “Get
your butt in gear to go where you want to go.” It’s very powerful motivation, but it only works if you know
what they’re trying to do. So don’t put your needs first, don’t put the business’s needs first, and don’t put your
frustrations first.
Put where they’re trying to go first. And then secondarily, from my perspective for leverage, introduce what
the business is trying to do for other people. So if you’re going to correct someone because they keep showing
up late, don’t say, “I’m mad at you and I’m going to punish you,” because that’s an authoritarian model of
the world that most people don’t want to live in, and don’t say, “You’re not serving the business’s needs and
that’s what you’re paid to do,” because again that puts them in a subservient kind of weird relationship to the
business and it says that they’re not as important and what have you.
But on the other hand, you say, “Our business is here to change lives and we’re trying to affect the world out
there and we’re trying to be as organized as we can. And on top of you not going in the direction you need to
go, we’re not serving people as well as we could. We’re not contributing to our customers. We’re not doing
the thing that we’re here to do as well when you show up late. We’re not helping other people the way we
need to help them.” It’s okay to introduce that because that’s kind of like taking group responsibility. It’s
making them part of the group and it’s talking about what they’re doing is resulting out in the world.
So keep your self-interest out of it and keep the business interest out of it. Do you remember the scene, if you
saw that old movie, The Matrix, where Neo wakes up in the morning and his alarm clock’s blaring and he’s
late and he shows up and it’s the guy sitting in the suit and the window washer is outside and the windows are
squeaky clean and you can tell it’s just a sterile, inhuman environment and he’s like, “Mr. Anderson, I think
you have problem with authority and that’s why you’re late. You have a problem with authority. Basically, you
think you’re the only one in the world.” Not, “I know where you’re trying to go and you waking up late isn’t
serving that and we’re serving people; that’s what we’re doing here.
We’re pulling as a team and when you’re not here pulling as a team, it doesn’t allow us to serve people as well.”

Different ends of the spectrum. Another thing that you can do is look at the trajectory of their life if you want
to like really dial this up. Look at where they say they want to be in the future. I like a one to three year time
frame; you can go outside of that if you’d like, but three years is a really nice distance because most people
can see that far in their future and they can see how the direction they’re going; their trajectory is going that
direction. If you can see a bigger future for them than they see for themselves – so if you know that right now
they make $50,000.00 per year and in three years, they’d like to be making $80,000.00 a year and if right now,
they manage two people and at that point, they’d like to be managing five people, you can say – only if you
believe this, but it’s not that hard to have a bigger vision for most people than they have for themselves.
If you say, “Honestly, I’m looking at what you’re doing right now and the trajectory you’re on and I think if
you keep doing this, you could be making $100,000.00 in three years and I think you could have an even
bigger team. I think you could be further along your career path if you just do this and this and learn these
three skills, and you know, I’m not guaranteeing anything because we don’t know, I mean this is all about
your performance – business environment has to be good and whatever, but I think you could be doing a lot
more than that.” Now you’re really getting on their side. Having a bigger vision for them than they have for
themselves is very powerful.
Not, “You know, in three years I think you could be making 27 billion dollars.” That’s ridiculous, but just a
little stretch goal, like a little bit more. So I mentioned this idea of having a bigger purpose, a bigger vision.
As people management leverage goes, whether it’s attracting people to come to work with your business,
whether it’s getting them behind a project and aligning them, managing them, keeping them motivated on a
regular basis, there’s nothing, in my experience, as motivating – over the long term, the long haul, not just in
the moment, but over the long term, as knowing a person’s future aspirations that they have for themselves
and even having a bigger vision for them (so you can keep coming back to that and keep tying to that and
keep using that as the tractor beam) and having the business have a bigger mission; to contribute to the world
in some way and make the world a better place.
Those two things together: knowing the person’s future aspirations (their future) and having the company
have a bigger vision – when you put those together, you can solve almost any problem. Almost any problem
and you can keep a group of people motivated long term. So we’re going to do an exercise. What you’re going
to do is you’re going to find a partner and you’re going to ask them what their aspirations are for the future.
You’re going to say, “Where would you like to be in three years, professionally?” So what would you like to be
doing? What will your role look like? How big will your business be? How much money will you be making?
Listen for their hot button.
Listen for the thing that you can tell is really the key for them. If it’s, “In three years, I want to be making
$175,000.00 a year so that I can put enough money so that my kids can go to college.” If that’s their hot
button, then that’s the thing you want to grab on to; not what you think their hot button is. But once you get
it, then say to them, “Is this it? Do I really have it here? Is the $175,000.00 so you can send the kids to college;
is that really the thing in three years? Yeah, okay good. Then say, “Okay, what is the one condition that you
can put in place right now in your life or your business? The one structure, the one condition that you can
put that will almost make it inevitable that you get there; that will just force that thing to happen?” Got it?
And then what we want to do is we want to tie these things together and we want to use this to motivate
the person. We want to say, “So you want to be at $175,000.00 a year in three years so you can start putting
enough money for kids to go to college and you want to put this structure in place so that that will make this
inevitable. What do you have to do? How is that going to work? How are we going to make sure that we get
you to $175,000.00 a year and how are we going to make sure that that structure gets put in place?” And just
get a feeling for – just get an experience of what it’s like to help another person figure out where their future is

at and what they need to do to get there and help them get clarity. What do we need to do to make sure that

clarify, helps them build trust.
Most people lack clarity; they like it. Did you get a feel for what it’s like to ask a person where they’re going

It’s creating kind of a container or context in which they can do the work. It’s a very powerful mindset. What
you’re doing when you’re doing that is you’re setting up conditions in their life that allow them to access their
own resources. You got that? You are becoming the condition in their life to help them access their own
resources.
When you figure out where they’re trying to go and then what they need to do, you get them to tell you what
they need to do; what’s the condition they need to put in place. And then once the bead is on it, then you can
put your input in and say, “Hey you might want to do this different, we can go to the next level here, I can add
something over here.” Once they put the big rocks in place, then you can bring in the pebbles and the gravel.

Eben Pagan: When you’re communicating with your team, communicating with people that work with
you, remember that most people are oriented towards problems. Most people are oriented toward problems.
If you’re a person who is oriented toward solutions or toward the future, which a lot of entrepreneurs I’ve
noticed tend to be, this can be very frustrating because you say, “Hey, let’s do this thing.” They say, “It’s not
possible,” or, “It can’t happen.” One of the things that is valuable to do if you can put this into your repertoire
is if you’re planning a project or embarking on some new direction in your business, ask the other person or
the team to share their thoughts about it.
As they say, “Here’s what’s wrong with it. Here’s what won’t work. Here are all the problems,” listen.
Something in there will be valuable to you. There will be something in there that will help you see in a
different way or understand things a little bit differently. More importantly, it’ll help them feel heard, and it’ll
build trust. I, admittedly, am not the best at this. But I try. I do the best that I can. I’m so future oriented
that I sometimes get a little frustrated with the, “Oh, this won’t work.” But for the sake of the other person, I
realize that that is in a lot of the way the value that they bring.
It’s going to be on their shoulders to get the job done. They’re actually going to be carrying the brunt of the
weight, and they don’t want to screw up. The reason why they’re looking at those things is they want to get
the job done right. I can see their good intention. This is a little bit autobiographical, but listen to them.
Allow them to share. As a gentleman that was the CEO for a friend of mine’s company a while back said, “If
they help plan the fight, they won’t fight the plan.” This is also something I’ve been doing a lot of work on
myself. As you’re getting the idea together and putting the concept together, get other people involved in it.
Get the input from other people on you team. Have them put in their two cents. When they put their two
cents in in the ideation phase, they feel involved and they are involved. When you show up and say, “Here’s
what we’re going to do,” they kind of feel like that’s your thing and not our thing. I’m as guilty of this as
anyone, but you can do much better. Also, keep communicating the vision and keep communicating the why.
Keep communicating the why. Why you’re doing it. My experience is that you can’t really do this too much.
You can’t keep synching it up too much.
You can’t zoom out and just keep re-synchronizing and reorienting too much. Everyone likes to know where
they’re at. If you’re in the middle of a project, and it’s a three-month project, and you’re at Day 45, say to the
team, “Ninety days ago, we set out to do this thing. The way it looks to me is we’re right on track right now.
When we get this project done, here is the affect it’s going to have on our business. Here are the things it’s
going to do for the world. We’re taking our business to the next level. We’re doing a great job. Keep going.”
Zoom out and reorient it. Keep talking about the why, why you’re doing it. Keep talking about why you’re
making a difference in the world and how you’re doing it. Share stories. Share from the front lines. One
of the things I try to do as much as I can remember to is talk about the conversations that I have here, and
what our clients and our family of customers are saying at our live events and the feedback that I get. I know
that the other 80 people back home that while we’re doing this right now are diligently answering customer
service e-mails and dealing with technical issues and really holding up the structure of our business, they
don’t have this experience.
They want to know that someone’s life changed because of what they did, and that they had a part in that.
Talk about the difference you’re making in the world, and bring specific anecdotal stories. Specific anecdotal
stories are very powerful and influential to our human brains. We’re wired to synch. We can relate to
seeing one other person do something. If I show up, and let’s say you’re thinking about donating a kidney to
someone in your family. You sit down and say, “Let me do a little research on this,” and you talk to two people.
One person says, “Ninety-eight out of 100 people that donate a kidney are just fine with no complications. It’s

going to be great.”

Usually, they’re doing – there is overlap in what they’re doing. Some person has three different skills, and

“We’ll give you this piece of software. You can use it for free, even though it costs $1 million. Or would you

who just tracks things and gets them done. I’ll take the $50,000 to $75,000.00 person all day long over the
world’s most sophisticated piece of project management software. Why is that? Because again, I’m dealing
with a small little Navy seal team that is getting things done.
The scenario I just said of having a group of ten people that’s going to launch a dozen products in about a year

That’s about what we’re doing. We’ve got about ten people. Me and about nine
