Tana, who you see out there. We get on the phone everyday. Tana has got the list. We just knock things out.
We make a lot of progress. I’ll take working with her at my side, helping my crazy, disorganized mind be
organized over any piece of software.
If you want a manager project, and you want to use a system and say, “What software do I use,” use a Google
doc. Open up a Google spreadsheet. Across the top, put the dates. Down the left side, put all the things
that need to be done. Put some Xs in the boxes, or put a little thing in here. “This is the date we need to get
it done by.” Or put someone’s name in the box of who is responsible for it. Get the people around and say,
“Here’s the project. It’s going to take 30 days to get this thing done. Fred, these are your things. You’re going
to do these. Here are your mile stones.”
Then get on the phone every day or in person, and have everybody update on how they’re doing, moving
toward their goals. A simple spreadsheet with everything on it that everyone needs to do and a list of things
and everyone updating and reporting and synching is much better than a piece of sophisticated project
management software. Again, especially in a small growing guru or information product business. If you’re

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 7
dealing with different types of people that are all working together on a small project team, I think it’s a great
idea to have deadlines and milestones that need to be accomplished, and have someone talking about them
all the time.
Highly structured organizer types, they like deadlines because they like to get things done in advance. They
like to manage the project so they’re ahead of the curve. They don’t like being behind the 8-ball. They don’t
want to improvise on everything. The creative types, and I call them organizers and creators in business, the
super creative types that thrive on chaos and never make any schedule or anything. They just show up and do
stuff and create magic.
They need the deadlines, or else they’ll never get anything done. Creatives are often perfectionists. They
look at their designs and products like art. They want to keep messing with it and tweaking it until it’s just
perfect. They need to know whatever you get done, you’ve got to be done by Tuesday. So Tuesday is when it
is, and they’re going to stay up all night on Monday to get it done on Tuesday. But it’s going to be in, and it’s
going to be done.
The organizers, they can look at something like, “Okay, we’ve got a 30 day deadline.” They work backwards,
and they can put all the other timelines in place, and they can figure out, “This is what I’ve got to do
tomorrow.” The creative types, if you say the deadline is in 30 days, they’re like, “That’s great. I get 29 days
to chill out.” It’s kind of where their minds go. It’s important to have the organized types set up a bunch of
milestones and deadlines so that the creative types have a deadline every day. Do you follow?
They need something. “So tomorrow, you’ve got to turn in this, and the day after that, you turn in this.” For
me, that’s kind of how I work. I’m always juggling a lot of things. I thrive on the chaos. I look at it and go,
“Tuesday we’ve got to turn in the copy for this thing so that on Thursday we can launch because it’s got to go
in the technology.” So then Tuesday, so then I’m looking at Monday. I better keep my evening free. That’s
kind of some reality there.
So deadlines are great. Lots of small little milestone deadlines are very valuable for the creative types. If
you’ve got a team of several people, it’s great to make a little rough outline with several milestones and
deadlines, and this one is coming up, and that one is coming up, and this one is coming up. Then give it
to the team, and let them own it. That’s how you can start to get out of micromanaging. You can just get
everyone to agree on when everything needs to be done, and then say, “If you need help, give me a call.”
Then all the little – if everyone is meeting their milestones, if everything is getting done, you’re on track.
If not, then you know that something is wrong. We’ll talk to this again in just a moment, but check in
constantly. Sync with people constantly. I want to give you three of what I consider to be the biggest people
management mistakes that we make in business in smaller businesses. No. 1 is imitating the practices of
huge companies. I think this is a mistake we not only make with people. We also make it with marketing and
everything else.
A lot of people say, “I’ve got three people on my team, so I’m going to look and see what Starbucks does.”
Starbucks has 30,000 people on their team. They’re in a completely different realm. They’re like another
species. What you want to ask is what did Starbucks do when they had three people? That’s the question
you want to ask. Don’t imitate what they’re doing now. Imitate what they did when they were your size if you
want to imitate someone else. Don’t imitate the practices of huge companies.
The first thing you need is not a giant employee manual with 50,000 policies and an org chart and a bunch
of titles and all that stuff. If you want to put those in place later, fine. But if you aren’t a professional, if you

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 7
don’t know how to use them, be very careful. The second mistake is giving status and power to someone that
hasn’t earned it over time. A lot of times small growing businesses will hire someone, and they’ll say, “You’re
going to be the COO.” As if they know what the COO was. “This person will feel important because they’re
the COO.”
Then the business starts growing, and the company gets to 17 people, and they realize, “I want to hire this
person that used to be a COO, and I’ve got this person over here who we call a COO. What do we make?
COOO? The super COO?” You get really screwed up. So when you copy big companies without thinking
it through, and then you give people status and power, you just hand it to them, that’s a big problem. Huge
problem. What I like to do is anyone that comes to work for our company – internally, in our business, we
don’t use titles.
We don’t have formal titles in our company. Yes, there has been a little bit of friction over it, but overall, I
think it has been a great decision to get us to the place we’re at right now. When there are titles, people start
using them and wearing them like little rank badges and saying, “I’m the this. I’m the COO.” When nobody
has a title and they just have to be themselves and their reputation is built on the results they get and what
their team performs, that’s when it’s more real. We hire somebody. We’ll hire a very senior person, someone
that’s been a COO at another company, in a real one.
I’ll say, “In our business, we don’t have any titles. You earn your reputation over time. For the first few
months, go to work helping this team become great.” If all goes as planned, they will eventually say, “Hey, you
should be the team leader over here on our team management course.” They usually do. They usually figure
it out very quickly. If you’ve made a good decision, the rest of your team will – this is exactly what we need to
lead us. They kind of almost elect to the person they help.
I’m talking – this is a little bit of an idealistic world, but the alternative is trying to give people power they
didn’t earn over time with other people. That creates long-term friction. If you’ve ever seen any of these what
they call the social org chart, they’ll take the org chart of a company, and then they’ll have someone go in and
actually make an org chart based on the social structures and who gets things done. There will be one person
down here in the middle with all these lines going to them.
They’re actually the one that knows how to get stuff done, and they’re the one that everyone goes to when
something needs to happen. They don’t have a big, fancy title. Humans, any two humans, when you put
them together, are a self-organizing system. They automatically start organizing into hierarchy structures.
That’s what we do. Humans are pack animals. We’re social animals. We’re political animals. We’ve naturally
organized. Within the first several seconds when two people meet, who is making eye contact with who, who
is looking away when the other one is talking, body language.
They’re already establishing social hierarchy. Whether you want to buy this or not, that’s pretty much what’s
going on with all of them. If you bring two people together and say, “You’re going to be the boss of you,” but
that’s not the way their inner animals are behaving, you’re going to have all kinds of weird tension. Who here
has ever had that feeling? You went to work for someone, for a boss, and the animal part of you knows that
if it were just you and them out on the Savannah in Africa 100,000 years ago that you would have killed them
and taken all of their food.
And they’re your boss. You just know right from the beginning that this is not going to work out. This isn’t
– yeah. So don’t do that with your team. Let people earn their status and power over time with each other,
and help them smooth out the rough edges so they can work together. So mistake No. 2 is giving status and
power that isn’t earned over time. No. 3, watching your hopes and fantasies instead of results. With another

Then we watch our hopes and fantasies instead of looking at their results. If they’re not getting results

When I hear that kind of thing, I’m like, “You know what? Three, six, nine, 12 months, this is probably
going to be a bad story because they’re so emotionally invested in the other person working out. I know that
they’re kind of blind to some things. Rose, who isn’t here who is a good friend of mine and does nutrition

Their favorite little craving and cheat foods, they like them so much because they activate the immune
system, and it makes them feel like they’re on drugs. They have no idea this is even going on. I don’t
remember how that relates to the story, but it’s interesting. Whatever. I’m going to give you four important
people management mindsets. This one comes from Jim Collins of Good to Great. First who, then what. My
interpretation of what he’s talking about applied to a small growing information product business is if you’re
going to do a project, you need to find the right person for the job. Somebody that has the correct skill set,
attitude, mindset, and then give them the thing to do.
What Jim Collins talks about is great companies. They first organize the people, and then they say what are
we going to do. You probably already know a little bit of the direction you’re going to go. Spend a lot of time
focused on the who. The who will take care of the what. If you get the right who, they know how to do stuff.
They know what to do. If you don’t have the right who, you’re never going to get there. You can’t get there
from here.
Another mindset, one strength, which is an internal talent someone has developed over time and turned into
mastery, is worth ten average skills. One strength, talent that has been trained, practiced, cultivated over
time, turned into a strength. Talent built into outward strength, is worth ten average skills. What a lot of
us do is we make a little job description of the person we’d like to hire, and we put everything we’d like them
to do on it. The problem with that is that it’s very rare that you’ll find a person who can do all that different
stuff. If you do find them, they’ll probably be average at all those things. They’ll probably just be average
period.
They’re not going to be really great at something. If you find somebody that’s amazing at an important
particular skill, and it’s their natural talent or strength, they’ll figure out how to get all the other stuff done.
Often, they’re pretty good at a bunch of other things as well. Don’t find someone that’s mediocre at a bunch
of things. Find someone that is just amazing at delivering in one particular area in life.
Mindset No. 3, hire expecting that a person will not be profitable for at least a year. The reality is that they
probably won’t, on average. They probably won’t be profitable for at least a year. If you’ve got an exploding
business, and you can just slot them in and they can go, maybe. When you do all the math and you really
look at the training time and how much you have to pay them and your time that you’re spending with them,
if you hire with the mindset that it’s going to be at least a year before this person is profitable, you’ll think

96

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 7
about hiring people differently.
You’ll know and realize that you’ll be putting a year into this person before they’re even going to become
profitable. I really have to be careful. No. 4, never underestimate your ability to idealize and excuse a person
who isn’t performing. I’d like to give you a few management practices that can be very valuable for you. The
first one comes from W. Edwards Deming. It goes like this. Drive out fear. This was a statistician that said
this. It was one of the most important things he taught in business. Drive out fear. When you feel the people
around you in fear, figure out how to get rid of it. You don’t drive out fear by saying, “If you don’t stop being
afraid, I’m going to kill you.”
That’s not the way it works. You have to create a safe environment. Drive out fear. Let people know that
it’s okay to fail. Encourage them to fail. We’ve been going through a really interesting cycle of this inside
our business lately as we’re looking at reinventing a whole bunch of different things in our world in new
business models, and launching totally new products. I’m seeing it come up. We’re trying to do a bunch of
new things. There is fear coming up. What I keep reminding our team is I say, “We’re awesome. Look at our
team. Look at how well we perform.”
We don’t screw up because we’re stretching ourselves. Our team rarely fails because I was trying to make
that huge jump, and I just didn’t quite go far enough. We usually screw up because we were thinking small.
We screw up because of some little thing, and we rarely even screw up. We don’t even screw up enough. So
encouraging your team and saying, “It’s totally okay if you screw up. Take some more risks. Go for it,” can be
very valuable.
It’s scary as the business owner because it’s your money that they’re taking risks with. But if you hire
superstars, you drive out fear, you figure out where they’re trying to go, and you get on the same page and
build trust, they will treat your money as if it was their money. They’ll really be sensitive to it. They usually
need to be encouraged to try more stuff to get outside the box. So drive out fear. Let them know that it’s okay
to make mistakes. Encourage them to go for it.
No. 2, praise in public, criticize in private. If you got criticism, negative feedback, or you’re upset at someone,
No. 1, tell them first. No. 2, tell them in private. Don’t air your stuff in front of the group. If somebody else
has a criticism, try not to say it in front of the group if you heard it from them. But praise people in public. If
you’ve got something positive to say about them, say it about them in public. Tell them you’re doing a great
job in front of other people.
A couple of distinctions for outsourcing and using professional services firms. Often times, when you’re
growing an information product business, it’s more economically viable to outsource the project and hire
somebody else to do it. If you need a logo designed for your business, it probably doesn’t make sense to go
hire a professional designer for $6,000.00 a month so they can make you a logo. Easier to outsource that.
Great website, by the way, you’ll hear me talk about is called 99 Designs. Who has used 99 Designs? Cool?
Right.
So 99Designs.com. I actually met the owner recently. Really interesting and cool guy. You go up there and
post your project for logo design or whatever you need designed. Then designers go, and on spec, they make
designs and post them. You pick the one you like, and then you pay the person for the design. You get to look
at a whole bunch of professional designers. They design for you essentially for free. You choose the one you
like and turn the whole model around.
As you can imagine, by the way, a lot of professional designers are very upset about 99 Designs. They don’t

97

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 7
like it at all. Welcome to the 21st Century. When you’re outsourcing, I recommend that you use really good
specifications. You get really clear about what you want. Make sure you get a budget, and make sure you
communicate often. Often times, business owners will outsource something and say, “You handle this.
You’re the professional. I just want it off my plate.”
They’ll think that because they outsourced it, they don’t have to worry about it anymore. In a way, you kind
of have to worry about it more. There’s a thing in outsourcing and using professional services firms called
creep. Some people call it scope. Creep. What this is is the scope of the project creeps. What consultants
and professional services firms, what they’re good at is talking people into hiring them. That’s what they’re
good at. They have to go from job to job to job. What they do is if the technique is called promising blue sky.
They say, “We can do anything. It’ll be great. We can do it cheap. We can get it done on time.” What they
know is you’ll want stuff you didn’t know that you wanted when you get started, but you’ll be so far into it that
you can’t back out, and when they add the add ons – do you follow? The scope of the project creeps outward
and gets bigger. Be very tight with your specifications. Know what you want, get them to commit to a cost,
and if anything changes, make sure that you’re on it.
If using a professional services firm, which is an ongoing relationship like an accountant or an attorney, that
kind of thing where they’re on retainer, remember that they bill you for everything. They’re going to bill you
every time they answer an e-mail or talk to you on the phone. They’re not going to call you up and say, “I
just wanted to let you know that it looks like you’re starting to e-mail and call more often. I’ve estimated the
trajectory of your bill, and it’s going to go up a little bit.”
They’re not going to call you up and help you to manage your budgeting with them. They’re just going to
keep charging you. You’ve got to keep asking how much it would cost to do this. If we go into this project,
how much more am I going to need to talk to you. Remember that that’s business there. It’s okay to ask
those questions. When I sit down with those types of firms, I ask questions like, “How do you get paid? How
much do you charge an hour? How often do we talk? What does the relationship look like? What happens if
we don’t like your service?”
Just get it all out there, and then keep communicating on a regular basis. I have paid the bill for you for this.
I’ve gotten the 40 or 50 multiple times $1,000.00 bill – that’s $40,000.00 to $50,000.00 bill from the attorney’s
office. I go, “What’s this?” They go, “It’s that opinion you wanted.” I’m like, “I thought we were going to talk
on the phone for a half hour. I didn’t know you’d farm this out to three other people in the firm, get them
talking on the phone, and double billing.” That’s reality.
Keep the reigns on every project, and say, “How much will this cost? What does this cost? What are the
implications here? How much extra is that? What’s the budget?” Put it in an e-mail so it’s in writing. We
have an agreement you’ll do this. The maximum we’ll spend is $1,000.00 on this project. If it looks like it’s
going to go over, you’ll let me know. Correct? Good. Tighten it down. This is not favors going back and
forth and a friend giving you a ride to the store. This is serious business. This is the real thing.
Get tight specifications, solid estimates, lots of communication with contractors, outsourcing, and
professional services firms. Especially those professional services firms. Another mindset that’s critical
here, I’m just going to slip this one in. Most people that start up a smaller business and have a small growing
business, they do what I call playing business, and not doing business. Don’t play business by saying, “Well,
I’ve got this new business idea, and I need to register every domain name. I need to get 27 trademarks,
copyrights, and patents. I’ve got to hire a bunch of attorneys and make sure nobody steals my name.”

98

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 7
If you do that, you’ll spend $50,000.00 or $100,000.00 before you’ve even seen if anyone wants your product.
Has anyone ever done this or knows someone that does? So you can see that this happens. On a regular
basis, people will say to me throughout my life, “I’ve started this business, and I’m really exited, and I’m going
for it. I’ve got this name.”
Usually the name is lame, which is always funny. They’re like, “I called up an attorney. A friend who had a
friend who does trademarks. I said I wanted to register my name so nobody else can have it. They said it’ll
be $27,000.00 to do this and form that and register it in these 27 categories and all this. Do you think that’s
a good investment? I don’t want anyone to steal my name.” I’m like, “No, you idiot. What are you talking
about? You don’t even know if anybody wants to buy what you’re selling, and your name is lame.”
Don’t play business. Don’t think you need fancy business cards and a fancy logo and a bunch of trademarks
registered and all that kind of thing. When you start using a name or logo, put the little TM next to it. You
can do that legally without registering it. When you create a document, you write something, and you put
it online or publish it anyway, it’s copyrighted the moment you create it. I’m not a lawyer, so you have to
get legal advice. That’s just my perspective on things, but that’s the way it works. Do you get additional
protection by registering a trademark or copyright? In some cases yes, but in some cases no.
The law is interesting and flexible depending on what your perspective is. Worrying about somebody else
stealing something, or worrying about looking like a big company and all that, that’s all playing business.
You don’t need to do that. Do business. If you want to spend your time and money doing something, talk to
people who would be potential customers, and see if you can talk them into buying what you’re selling oneon-one. If you can’t, then get onto something else. Don’t get too attached to the name of a product or name
of a business.
Most names people choose, especially in the beginning, are not that good. They’re actually counterproductive
to the business. Don’t get attached. People firing practices. Firing people. No. 1. If you’ve got a C player,
let them go. It’s better for you. It’s better for the business. It’s better for them. It’s better for everybody, and
better for the customer. First, it should never be a surprise. If you’re sitting down with someone and saying,
“You’re fired. Goodbye,” and they didn’t know it was coming, there’s a problem.
You should be taking notes, keeping records, coaching them, letting them know where their performance
isn’t working over time. If it isn’t working out and isn’t working, great.
You should also keep records of this stuff. Ultimately, you might be sitting in front of a judge. You want
to make sure everything is done right. No. 2, you’re responsible, so act like it. It’s your business. You
hired them. You’re the professional hirer. From one perspective, they’re doing their job, and from their
perspective, they’re probably doing the best they can. If it didn’t work out, you’re responsible, so behave like
you are. Take responsibility for the situation as you’re talking to them. Help them.
No. 3, make sure you’re doing it with their best interest in mind. Look at the situation, and see how it’s
not working for them. Help them move on to something that’s working better for them. When you say to
someone, “You’re screwing up, so you’re fired,” that doesn’t serve everyone. On the other hand, when you say,
“In this particular role, things just aren’t working out. We tried a lot of different things. It’s not happening. I
hired you and put you in that role. I feel responsible, but this isn’t working out for you. I want something to
work for you, and here it’s not working, so let’s help you get something else.”
If they thank you for helping them when they leave, that’s how you know you’re doing the right thing. This
is a perfect world. It doesn’t always happen this way. Almost every person that has ever left working with

Eben Pagan: Let’s talk about managing money. Most of the people in here will be going through my selfmade wealth program as a result of being in a mission. We’re going to find a way to make sure that everyone
in the Guru Mastermind gets that as well. It’s going to be pretty awesome. Who has gone through the first
sessions already? What do you think so far? It’s a little on the brain twisting side, but it’s very important
stuff. A little bit of the psychology of money. Remember, we’re wired for a time that doesn’t exist anymore.
We live in an environment that we have almost completely terraformed or artificially created.
Money is one of the things that exists in this reality that we’ve created. Money is a symbol. It’s not the
real thing. One of the fascinating things about being human is we’re the symbol using animal. We’re the
symbolizing animal. Words are symbols. They’re not the real thing. What we often do is use words in our
heads, and we think we’re operating on the real thing. We logic it out, and we think that’s the way it works
because we made the words work in our heads. Money is similar in that way in that it’s a symbol. Money no
longer has intrinsic value inside of itself anymore.
Its value is as a medium of transacting. In the past, 1,000 or 2,000 years ago, you’d have some money. It’d
be gold or silver, and there’d be value in the gold that’s in the money. If the currency went back, you could
always melt the gold down, make some earrings, or do something with the gold. A guy named John Law back
in the 1700s in France who was a criminal figured out you could make paper money that didn’t have anything
backing it, and humans would take it and use it as if it was the real thing.
Massive innovation, so to speak. He became very wealthy as a result of this realization. Countries figured
this out and have started doing it a lot. I personally think we’re on a little shifty ground with that kind of
thing. Once you get a little of it – what I ask is if you had a printing press and were an ethical person, and
it printed $100.00 bills, and you had it in the basement, would you use it once in a while? You’re an ethical
person, though. Would you use it when emergencies came up? Most people would.
In some of the new psychological experiments going on, we’re starting to realize that most humans have
flexible ethics. They’re situational. Most of us like to think we’re solid and honest. Depending on the
circumstances. Money is a weird thing in this way. Countries seem to like to print a lot of it and get into
debt. Humans seem to get into debt and get their hands on as much as they can. We’ve got to remember
it’s not the real thing. When we’re using money, there is essentially a use tax involved when we use money.
Write this down. Using money costs money.
Whenever you translate or transfer an asset or something you have into money, you actually lose a little
of the value. Using money costs money. Another way I like to look at is that money has a hole in its own
bucket. When you have your value in money, some of it is leaking out automatically. Just simply as a result
of inflation, and inflation is increasing the money supply, so it keeps getting diluted. As we focus on getting
money, and we say, “How do I get more money?” We focus on that as the value. We dilute ourselves. We
kind of screw up our homing system for value and assets. We start thinking money is the value and the asset.
It’s not.
Money is to assets what words are to the thing they represent. By focusing on just getting money, it’s kind of
like going to a restaurant and seeing a picture of a beautiful salad and going, “I want to eat that,” taking and
cutting out the picture, and eating it, and going, “That doesn’t taste like it looks.” That’s a little bit more of a
realistic way to think about money. It does have value in certain circumstances, but it fluctuates over time.
Another interesting insight about money that I’ve learned is from Joe Sugarman. He talks about the idea of
how if you want something to grow, expand, or develop, put your attention and focus on it.
Where you put your attention, that’s what grows in your life. That’s what expands. If you put your attention

108

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 8
on your health, your health will tend to expand. What happens if you put your focus and attention on
money? Most people would think money expands. It doesn’t. Why doesn’t it expand? Because it’s not the
real thing.
It’s like saying you put your focus on a word. A word is just a symbol. If you put your focus and attention
on creating value and creating assets, those are the things you can trade in for money any time you want.
You following me? If you’ve got a bar of gold, you can trade that in for money. If you have a business that’s
throwing off cash, you can trade that for money. The wealthy, that’s what they do is focus on assets. You’ll
notice when you read the list of Forbes 400, and this person is worth $3 billion and this person is worth $7
billion, none of them have their wealth in money.
They don’t have their wealth in cash. It’s all estimated. They have to figure out how much money it would
be worth. I think it’s funny that they say they’re worth $7 billion. They’re actually worth something real, and
not money. If they liquidated everything, it might result in about $7 billion. The wealthy are smart enough to
not focus on money. When they get value, they put it into assets like real estate, stock companies, and things
like this that have real, tangible value.
Those of us that don’t have much wealth and haven’t created prosperity, what most of us do is focus on
getting money. When we get some of it, instead of taking it and converting it into assets that will grow in
value, we go burn it on things. We blow it on things that have no or low value, or things that are liabilities.
Robert Kiasaki – he calls them doo-dads. A lot of people think, “If I go buy a boat for $50,000.00, I’m going to
have an asset because that boat has value.”
The reality is when you get the boat, it loses 40 percent of its value as soon as you buy it new. Then the
maintenance cost of this thing, and the hassle of where you’ll store it and time, it’s a money suck. It turns
into a liability. Remember that wealth and assets can always be turned into money, but money is rarely
turned into wealth and assets. If you’ve got some money, if you get some, instead of saying, “Great, I got some
money. How do I hold onto this money?” Ask, “How do I turn this money into assets and wealth that will
grow in value over time and I can trade in for money any time I want?”
In business, this is critically important because when you start a business, you start realizing that you’re
creating things that have more real value. As you start looking at your business, you need to start looking at
value in a different way, with a different set of lenses on. In my reality, to me, the biggest profit in business is
learning, connecting a group of people together that learn and produce results together, the company’s core
competencies, the talent, the assets we’ve created, the product line, the marketing, distribution relationships.
These things are assets to me. They have value. The money is just kind of a result of those things. We want
to turn the chicken and egg thing around as we’re building a business. Realize that the objective is not to get
money. Money just happens to be the meter or the way we measure, and the way we trade one type of value
for another. When we’re trading one type of value for another, putting it into money costs money. What
people that don’t understand this philosophy do is they’re focused on the money and getting some money.
They go and they work, and then they take their paycheck and pay a bunch in taxes. They take some of the
take home pay, and they go pay some of their expenses. They take what’s left, and they maybe put it in a
savings account at 3 percent. What the wealthy do is have an asset, like a piece of income real estate. They’ll
sell it. They’ll use on of these magic loopholes that exist out there called a 1031 exchange where they’ll take all
the money that comes from the sale, and they have a window where they can roll that into a more expensive
property, and they pay no taxes on the gain.

109

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 8
You continue to defer taxes until you finally sell the last piece. If you understand compounding interest
and the way these types of things work, you realize that if you were to sell it and then pay the taxes and go
reinvest, then sell that and go pay taxes and go reinvest that the loss of momentum taking that bite out every
time when calculated over ten, 20, 30, 50 years or a lifetime is dramatic. The difference between the results
are huge. In a business, it’s important to learn to see what you might call expenseable assets. Learn to think
in terms of things that you can buy that you’re not skimming some of the money off the top before you buy it.
Instead of hiring someone to do some work for the business, I try to think, “How do we hire someone to
come work with us that will become a long term asset? They’ll be a producer and a creator of more value.”
I’d rather take – Robert Kiasaki points out that the best investment for a business owner is usually their
business because they understand their business. What blows my mind – this one just shocks me all the
time. Someone that’s a professional in some area of business, entrepreneur, growing a business, making
substantial money, making $1 million a year, will take $300,000.00 a year, and will go invest in stocks they
don’t understand.
They’ll be playing in some segment of the stock market that they don’t even get, and losing a lot of money
most of the time. What’s going on? Why aren’t you investing that money in your business? To get the
$300,000.00, you probably had to take $600,000.00 out to get the $300,000.00 after taxes and everything else
to go invest it in something that you don’t understand. Warren Buffett says we won’t invest in anything we
don’t understand. That’s why they only invest in very simple business for the most part.
Don’t invest in things that they don’t understand. Everyone in the late ‘90s was saying to Warren Buffett,
“You’re a fool. How could you not be getting involved in the technology boom?” He said, “We don’t know
anything about it.” In fact, Warren Buffett and Charlie Munger say, “We’re looking for one good investment
idea per year.” That’s their model. One good investment idea per year. They’re looking for something that
they understand that they get that is a good opportunity, and they’re in the right place.
They look at a lot of things, so they’re very selective. There’s a little bit of philosophy. Here are some
practices when managing your money and your business. No. 1, track your daily deposits and your daily
spending. We’ll talk about dashboards here in a little bit. Track them every day. Track them visually. It’s so
simple to log into your bank account every day and say, “Here’s how much money was deposited, and here’s
how much money was spent.” Take those numbers, and put them in a couple cells on a Google or Excel
spreadsheet. Track it every day.
Then run a moving average through it. Run a seven or a 15-day moving average to smooth it out. Count
for weekends and various things like that. Why do we want to track every day? Because it’s a feedback
mechanism. We see what’s going on, and it allows us to see the direction things are going. Most people don’t
track their deposits and spending really ever, visually. They wait until the end of the quarter. Most people
wait until the end of the year to really figure out what they made and what they lost. They’re not watching it
real time. Watching real time is key to get into the flow and rhythm.
No. 2, track the ROI of every investment that you make. Specifically, you want to track all of your marketing
and not invest in marketing that you can’t track. If someone comes to you and says, “Buy this marketing, this
branding, and you’re going to get your name out there.” You say, “Can I track it?” If they say, “No,” then say,
“I’m not interested.” Track it all. These days, you basically can track all of it. If you can’t measure the return,
you don’t know how to improve it. You don’t know how to tweak it. It’s basically a drug, for the most part.
Track all your investments and track the return you got.
Be disciplined about it. That’s what business is about. I think one of the reasons most people fail in business

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 8
is because they’re not disciplined about what they’re doing. They’re not treating it like a business. They’re
not managing investment and return. They’re not thinking about every dollar they spend as something that

bringing the old job mindset of, “I showed up. I did what I was supposed to do, and I didn’t get the results.
People don’t like me. I’m unlucky.” They’re just not tracking.
They’re not making everything accountable. Next, don’t invest in something big until you’ve seen a proof
of concept small. Don’t spend a lot of money unless you’ve spent a little bit of money, and it worked at that
level. Whenever we’re doing some type of a deal with a new partner or buying banner ads from a site, I want
to know what is the smallest amount of money I can invest so I can dip a piece of litmus paper into traffic and
see how responsive it is. I need to know something. If there’s a – because large companies and list brokers
and whatever, they figure this out. They know that marketers want to test.
They figured out a way to kind of fight back. You really have to pay attention. There’s a process in the direct
mail industry called hotline leads. You’ll go to a company and say, “I’d like spend – we’ve got a big budget.
We’d like to – we’re looking for partners, so we have a big budget here. Can we test your traffic? Can I buy
$5,000.00 or $500.00 worth of your traffic so we can test it out and see what the quality is?” They’ll say, “Sure.”
What they’ll do is send you their best, newest leads, which convert really well. You’ll go, “Wow, we spend
$5,000.00, and we got $25,000.00 in return. Let’s send them all our money.” Then over time, results go down.
Because most businesses don’t track long term and don’t keep their eye on the ball long term, they don’t
notice that that’s happening. It just kind of gets blended in to everything else that’s going on. You wake up
with a big giant hole in your bucket. You’ve got to keep tracking.
Even if someone has been a partner and sending you traffic for five years, you still need to keep watching
all the traffic on a regular basis. Here’s why. We were working with a big partner who was one of the top
websites on the internet with tons of traffic. Very profitable leads came into our business. It was a great deal
for many years. All the sudden, our volume went way up. It was like a bonus. We love these guys. It’s great.
We took a look, and it turned out that the quality went way down when we examined it. The volume went
way up, but the quality went way down.
We’re paying the same amount per lead, which means our bill is going way up, but our sales aren’t going way
up. What they did was they did a partnership with a much bigger website that sent them a ton of low-quality
traffic, and then they sent us a bunch of that traffic. If we weren’t paying attention, if we weren’t watching
and were a traditional company, whoever the sales manager was would have said, “We just doubled the traffic.
Give me a bonus.”
That’s the way people think, and that’s the way businesses operate. “I’ve made my metrics. I doubled our
traffic.” That’s the kind of stuff that can crash your business. Don’t invest until you’ve seen proof of concept,
and keep watching over time. You must track and watch all of your investments. When you’re interacting in
the business world with people, remember that they’re all focused on cash. They’re not thinking build assets,
build long-term relationships.
They are, kind of, but they’re thinking cash. Cash really talks. If you can show people money, a lot of
times, that’s more powerful than anything else you can do is to send them a check, give them some money,
and sometimes pay them in advance up front. We’ve had experiences where we want to do a partnership
with someone. We’ll be talking about it, and we can tell they’re kind of big. They normally wouldn’t talk
to someone of our size, but we want to get in the door, so we just send them a check for $2,500.00. We say,
“We’re just going to buy some media from you. We don’t how this will work out, but here’s the money.”

It speaks very loudly. Pay people what you owe them. Get it to them. Most businesses are used to having
to chase people down for their money and that kind of thing. Pay people when you owe them money. Very

“I can see that if we double and are going to $200,000.00, I’m not gong to make $50,000.00, I’m going to make
$100,000.00 in profit because it scales up. We’ve got all our fixed costs paid.” In theory, that works, but in
practice, it’s not usually so. Be careful with your estimates, and always keep your eye on them. My experience
is that as a business scales up, it actually becomes less profitable in most cases. You have to trade profitability
a lot of times for scale. Not always, but that’s my experience.
If you have your eyes set on the prize and think, “If we just invest this money, it’ll work out great,” and you

computer system would break because we couldn’t handle the volume. There’s $100,000.00 we have to spend.
We have to hire these 27 other people because didn’t know this whole other system would have to be put in
place.” As businesses scale, they usually become less profitable over time.
Let’s talk about dashboards. One of the most valuable things you can give yourself is real time feedback on
what’s happening in your business. I think the visual display of quantitative information, of numbers, is one
of the biggest inventions that humans have created. Charts, graphs, but only if you know how to use them.
W. Edwards Deming, who is the great management philosopher and consultant, he advised to create run
charts of all your numbers and to track them over time. Daily is, I think, the best average amount of time to
use.
Take all your key metrics, and open up a spreadsheet, and put all the dates down the left hand side, and across
the top, put the different metrics. Put the numbers in the box every day. Then after 30 days or so, select them
all or just click the letter at the top of the box. Click the charting button, and make a run chart, a little line
chart that shows the variation over time. Here’s why. In the real world, there’s something call variation. The
physical world is called physical variation. What that means is that any process that you track over time will
have variation in it.
If I’m a lathe operator in a machine shop, and I’m spinning discs and turning them into bowls, there’s going
to be variation in how long it takes me to get them done, how close to specification they are, how much wear
and tear is on the machine over time. No matter how tightly we fine-tune this process, there will always
be variation. Physical stuff out here in reality gets in the way. Parts break. People get sick. The air causes
friction. Does that make sense. There’s variation. Things go up and down.
You’ll also notice that, in sales, if you’re selling information products, there’s a daily cycle that you’ll find
where, during a certain of day, sales will come up and then go back down. Usually, it’s they come in the
middle of the day. Sales go up while people are awake, and they go back down at night. There’s also a weekly
cycle. Most people that sell information products will find that the beginning of the week coming into
Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, sales go up. Then they kind of come back down. Then you’ll find that there’s
probably a monthly cycle.
Some of our businesses, towards the middle of the month, sales go up. Then they come back down. There’s
also seasonality. You’ll find certain months do well, and certain months don’t do well. Often times, around
the holidays, sales kind of take a dip, and then at the beginning of the year, they kind of come back up. All
these are happening all the time whether or not you’re conscious of them. If you’re not creating run charts, if

you’re not tracking visually and looking at it and smoothing with moving averages, what you get is you come

If you create a visual dashboard – you’ve heard Alex come up here and talk a little bit about stripping his 80

The first metric is traffic. How many people visited your website? The second metric is leads or subscribers,
people that opted in and gave you their information. The third metric is sales. You can do how many sales

or 43 percent or 6 percent.”
You know that, and you can also start doing some rough calculations and figuring out how much every person
that visits your website is worth, and you can also do some rough calculations. How much is every subscriber
you get worth. You can do some rough calculations. How much is every customer worth? With those three
metrics, you can figure a lot of very important numbers in your business.
The next two are the ones we already talked about, which is daily deposits and daily expenses. Money that

to make this dashboard, you’re doing something wrong. You’re screwing up if it takes you more than ten. If it

would appear on the box here in the lower right corner. What Microsoft Excel is saying is, “I know what

Deming talked about two different types of variation. There are what are called common causes of variation

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 8
Two of the biggest mistakes in business are No. 1, thinking that a common cause is a special cause, No. 2,
thinking that a special cause is a common cause. Common causes are variation are the physical variation.
Just the variation that comes, the cycles of buying and selling, the patterns of behavior. It was sunny today,
so more people bought. Variation. Every once in a while, there’s a special cause of variation. Special cause of
variation is not a regular, common, recurring thing. It’s something new, like the technology stopped working,
or the website did a deal with a bigger website, and all the sudden, more traffic is coming in.
If you’re not doing this, if you’re not tracking visually, it’s much harder to see when things change. It’s much
harder to see when things change. What’ll happen is let’s say everything seems consistent in your business.
Then you feel like you’ve got a few down days. You’re like, “You know what, it’s just a few down days.” But
then things kind of seem to stay down. You can’t really – your brain isn’t seeing how down it is. It’s just that
you don’t pay attention. Then you find out long afterwards that maybe there was something wrong.
When you’ve got the chart, what happens is you’re looking at the chart, and sales are moving along and
tracking. Then all the sudden, there’s another thing that happens. You see that the game changed. You see
something happened that was not the same. When this kind of thing happens, you go looking to try to figure
out what happened. Anyone here ever had a link break on their website, or they made some change and they
weren’t able to buy from some traffic source for three months, and then you figured it out later? Who has
ever had that one happen?
That’s how you figure this stuff out. It happens, and instead of just looking at the column of numbers and
going – you can see. Something is different there. Something happened and changed. Most business people
don’t do this. They don’t visually track the trends of all the numbers and then run moving averages through it
so that when something changes, they go, “Something changed. I need to go figure out what that is.” There’s
a special cause of variation in the system, and I’ve got to go hunt that up.
The great thing about simple dashboards like this is they can be updated and managed in five or ten minutes
a day. Once you get it set up, you can outsource it to somebody. So the total cost of running one of these

day thing. They just log in, fill in all the numbers, and on to the next thing.
You’ve got to track. You’ve got to know your numbers. You’ve got to know what’s going on. Then once you
do this and see how powerful it is and you realize how it affects your behavior and how it motivates you and
how you get control of your business. You do this for 90 days, and you’ll have a feeling of security in your
business of just understanding it and knowing where it’s at that didn’t exist before. You’ll understand a
stability. You’ll have a real gut level feeling of, “Okay, this is a solid thing. This is something that’s consistent
and predictable.”
Once you do it, you’ll start realizing, “Oh, I can do this with everything. I can do this with the different

are. I can see them compared to each other on the same chart visually.” It’s very simple to do all these things

Then you start thinking like a businessperson. You really start abstracting out. Like we talked about earlier

you want. In our business, we track 100 different numbers.

Okay, we’re doing it. Let me give you some mindsets, and I’ll give you a few practical applications of
negotiation. First thing to remember in negotiation is that business is not fair. Do not expect it to be. Life

116

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 8
isn’t fair. Don’t expect it to be either. If life was fair or business was fair, you wouldn’t be able to have all the
advantages you do. If everything was fair, you wouldn’t be able to accelerate your success because you just
have to deal with the average and get what the average was. It’s a massive advantage that life and business
aren’t fair.
It is where advantage flows from is that reality. If you go into negotiations and business situations with,
“Oh, it’s not fair,” and look at how it’s unfair to you in a negative way, it’ll keep you down. But if you realize it
means you have all kinds of advantages, then you can use that as power to succeed. So remember, it’s not fair.
Don’t expect it to be. Stop trying to negotiate fair things. Fairness, as far as I’m concerned, is usually halfassed. We can do much better than fair for them and for us.
Also remember that the deal you make is often where the money is made or lost in the deal. If you make a
bad deal, that’s the inflection point. That’s the new vector. It’s like rocket telemetry on the way to the moon.
If you get the telemetry off a little bit, you can wind up three days away from the moon. You don’t want to
do that. The deal is important. It’s worth extra time, extra work, and extra negotiation in figuring it out.
Another mindset of negotiation – Steven Cubby brings this idea. He calls it win/win or no deal. It’s the idea
that we’re both going to win, or else, we’re going to walk away. Either one of those is fine with me.
I’m totally okay with it. If we don’t come to a deal, great. I used to kind of feel obligated. If I start talking to
someone about working with me, or if I start talking to someone about potentially doing business, I used to
feel like I’d put myself in the toboggan sled. What’s it called? The slope? The slalom. I’ve put myself in the
toboggan sled slalom of completion. Now we’re just going to pick up speed, and I’m going to have to do it.
There’s all this obligation. What I realized now is, no, most of those are going to be no deals.
Most of the initial prospecting conversations or exploratory conversations are going to be no deal. That’s
fine. I’ll probably have made a friend, we’ll exchange some value, and there will be an opportunity to do one
later. They may introduce me to someone who’ll be better, or I might introduce them to someone who would
be better. Win/win or no deal. We’re both going to win, or there’s no deal. Within win/win or no deal, it’s
important to remember that the other guy is going to try to get the best deal that they can in almost every
situation.
Whatever deal they wind up with, if they take it, they’re going to say, “Okay, that’s the deal.” They’re going
to go on. To them, it was a good deal. A good deal, there’s so much leeway between what a good deal is and
what it’s not. One of the dirty little secrets of the internet and internet advertising and marketing is that
many large media outlets are selling banner ads over here at $20.00 CPMs, which means you pay $20.00, and
they show your banner ad 1,000 times. Over here, they’re selling the same banner space, same inventory, for
50-cent CPMs. It’s the same thing.
Some people are paying $20.00 CPMs, and some people are paying 50-cent CPMs. If you call the person
selling them for $20.00, they’ll negotiate with you down to $17.00. If you call the person who is selling them
for 50 cents, they’ll negotiate with you down to 35 cents. Lots of variation. There’s a lot of leeway. There are
a lot of different things happening out there. If you can get a good deal, and the other person – it’s a win for
them. Don’t get too weirded out by it. Get good deals for you and your business because your business needs
to survive.
Another thing. Remember that I said win/win or no deal. Communicate often that no deal is absolutely fine.
Say it. Bring it up. Say things like, “If this works out, then it’ll be good because we can do this. If this works
out.” It’s not certain it’s going to. Or say, “Hey, I’ve been looking at numbers here, and I don’t know. Your
pricing looks a little high. I don’t think this is a fit for us, but I’d like to explore further just in case it is.” Say

things that indicate this might not work, and you’re perfectly okay with that.
Something negotiation teachers say that they teach is negotiate with one hand on the door. In other
words, communicate that I’m about to leave. That’s okay. That’s fine. Another thing about negotiation I’ve
discovered is don’t fear time. Sometimes timing is everything. We’ll talk about some opportunities where
you an use time to your advantage, but scarcity is a negotiation tactic used all the time. Marketing, sales, it’s
really used all the time.
It turns out that human beings get all freaked out when there is a scarce supply of something. You can use it
ethically to sell, and it can be a powerful motivator. But if there’s a deal on the table, don’t fear that it’s going
to be gone. I really feel the way to approach life is that if you’re looking at a deal, the deal is on the table, and
you really want it, but it’s not 100 percent. It’s not like I should just take this because it’s absolutely solid, and
we’ve got it all figured out. It’s kind of in that gray zone, which most of them are.
Just say to yourself, “If this doesn’t work out, there’s a better one down the road, and this one will probably be
available later.” My experience is that when someone says, “You know what? We’ve got this ad inventory, and
we can give it to you for half price. You usually buy this much. If you buy four times as much, we’ll only sell
it to you for twice as much money.” You go, “You know what? Give me a while to think about it.” What you’ll
often find out is the reason they said that is because the company is going bankrupt. They’re about to go
under, and next week it’ll be an even better price.
No matter who good it sounds or how much scarcity, things are usually available down the road. I have a
friend who was looking at a piece of real estate. Things kind of happened where I think I want this, but I
don’t know. The scarcity thing came up. It might be gone. They said, “I’ve just got to commit to it.” I was
saying, “In this market, I’ll bet you could get this much of a discount. This is probably what the going rate is.
This is what you could probably make happen.” They just didn’t do it because a fear that the deal wouldn’t be
available.
Don’t fear time. Don’t worry about it. It’s usually on your side. If you lose one once in a while, it’ll probably
be one in five or one in three. It’s fine. It’s okay. When you’re staring down the mouth of a deal with a person
saying, “This might be gone,” and you say, “That’s okay. There’ll probably be a better one around the corner.”
That’s when they realize they don’t have leverage and can’t use that on you. That’s when you often find out
what is really happening. There’s usually a better deal on the other side of it. Something else can work.
Something I’m always looking for when negotiating is I’m always looking for what I call the alternate
currency. Most people that are negotiating to buy or sell something are focused on the money part because
they’ve become convinced that money is valuable and that money is where the value is. They’re always
focused on the price. This, and let’s go with this much money on that. Really, the transaction is happening
for some reason other than money. The other person or the other party wants the money to do something
else.
If – let’s say you’re talking to a website where there could be some great distribution where you can really do
well. You can do a big deal with them. You’re looking at the price, and it’s just a little too high. It just doesn’t
make sense. Ask yourself, “What are these guys trying to do?” You might figure out that what they’re trying
to do is get the money so that they can go and hire some people to write more content for them. They pay
their content writers a lot of money.
So what they need is content. So how about this? I’ll give you a lower amount of money, and I’ll write some
content for your website. They may say, “That would be great. That’s exactly what we wanted.” You’ll realize

that by putting your content on their website, you’ll actually get higher quality traffic than if you just run ads.
Those types of opportunities are just available in almost every situation if you look for the alternate currency.
What is the other person or business really trying to do? What’s their big problem? Bring some of that to
bare on the deal making.
Another tip for negotiation. When the other person is talking, you’re winning. When the other person is

party in the beginning.
I went and talked to another party. During a conversation with one of the other parties, somebody else that
was with me was talking to them, and I was listening. They made a comment off handed that they basically
just didn’t realize how they were communicating just how flexible they really were. Does that make sense? It

Once I understand what their reality is and what they’re trying to do, then I can create something that’s as

People will do that. Someone came up to me at one of the breaks here and was talking about mentors and

We’re in a conversation, and they mention something about certain placement of deals. I just threw out a

what is no big deal.” So get in front of people and talk to them.
Another huge leverage point is eating with someone you’re negotiating with. Eating food together with
another human being changes the whole vibe. If you have a conversation with someone when they’re
hungry before lunch versus while they’re eating and they’re getting their needs met, it can be two completely
different conversations. Anyone here who is married knows exactly what I’m talking about.
Another great technique we use when negotiating is ask what the high volume price is as the first question.
If you call up Microsoft and want to advertise on MSN and buy some banner ads, you don’t call up Microsoft
and say, “I’ve got $75.00, and I’d like to buy some banner ads.” You call and say, “I’d like to get your pricing.”
Talk to the most senior person you can. You want to come up with the most optimistic and yet realistic
potential volume that you could consume with them.
If you’ve done the math on some other banner ads you’ve purchased, and you realize that scale would work,
and let’s say your business does $100,000.00 a year, you want to say, “We’ve tested banner ads in a few other
places. They seem to be working very well. We’ve got a scalable product. It’s an electronic, digitally delivered
product. If banner ads work with you, I’d love to be spending $50,000.00 to $100,000.00 a month.” Because
you would if it worked. But be realistic. I’m not saying to lie. I’m saying to actually figure out at scale how
much could it be worth.

Then say, “I’d like to know what your pricing is.” They’ll come back and tell you what the pricing is.” Say,

The transition times, the end of the month, end of the quarter, end of the year. These are when big windows
of opportunity open up because sales people need to make their quotas. Companies need to make their
quarters. Things become available that no one knows about. Sometimes they’ll call you up and bug you,

Your representative is looking at their bonus, and if they do a deal with you, they get to book the revenue

Different people think different about what money and value exchange is. I once tried to negotiate with
someone to get a better deal buying more volume.
Instead of sending him $10,000.00 for the volume I wanted, I was like, “I tell you what. I’ll give you $8,000.00

about it. We came to a deal where I still sent him the $10,000.00, but he gave me bonus placement that was
worth more. I actually wound up getting a better deal. He just had his eye on the $10,000.00. That’s all he
cared about. He was like, “More volume? Fine. We can give you more leads. I need the $10,000.00.”
Asking in different ways, and sometimes it can be terms, pricing, overtime. Sometimes a website will be like,

can just run your banner anytime when we want, I can give it to you for 20 percent of the cost.” Just ask lots of
questions, be flexible, and remember that there are many different ways to skin the cat.
This is a weird freak of human psychology that I discovered in negotiation years ago before I ever really got
into business. It was with a friend of mine, who unfortunately, is no longer alive. We’d buy and sell a lot of
guitar and electronic equipment from each other. More like we’d like actually horse trade. There wasn’t a lot
of buying and selling going on. He kind of knew me as being a little bit on the shrewd side. So I would never
make a bad deal with someone else. We’d negotiate the thing back and forth. “I’ll trade you this guitar for
those two amps and a couple of packs of strings,” or whatever.
One time, we negotiated back and forth. We came to a deal. I’m like, “Let’s do it.” He’s like, “I don’t know if
I want to.” I was like, “What do you mean?” He said, “If you’re saying yes to this, this must be a bad deal for
me.” I’m like, “Dude, come on. We’re friends.” What I figured out at that point was that if when I’m ready
to close the deal, if I ask for one more little thing from that person, they’ll feel better about the deal than if I
just say yes. You know what? It has served me very well over time. People tend to not appreciate things they
don’t have to work for.
Try to close the deal with one extra little thing. They talked you into it. The reason why is they get to
win. They get to get what they wanted. They get to be the one that made the call when they do it. In an
interesting later experiment, I went to a big retail outlet. This was probably ten years ago or something. I
was buying this acoustic guitar. I went there and picked this thing up. I liked it. It had a crack in the

120

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 8
headstock where the tuning keys were. Any crack there is bad news. There’s something tainted about these
instruments.
But it was in a certain – it was like the headstock was this way, and it was this way up at the top. It wasn’t
at a bad place where it could break. I know guitars, and I wasn’t going to – this wasn’t a museum piece. I
was actually going to play this. I knew I could get a good deal on it. I started negotiating. Whenever I see
things like that, I’m like, “I’m about to get an amazing deal.” I wish I could remember all the details now, but
this is the way it went down. The guitar was a $700.00 guitar, and I knew they probably paid $350.00 for it
wholesale.
I was like, “I’ll give you $300.00 for it.” The guy goes to his manager and is like, “No, you know what? We
can’t do any lower than $325.00.” I said, “I tell you what. I’ll give you $300.00, and you give me the guitar
and a pack of strings.” He was like, “Let me see what I can do.” He comes back, and he’s like, “No, we can go
$320.00, but that’s as low as we can go.” I’m like, “I’ll tell you what, $300.00, you get me two packs of strings,
and I also want some picks.”
“Let me see what I can do.” He goes back, and he comes back, and he’s like, “I don’t think we can go any
lower.” I’m like, “Okay, $300.00, a box of strings, ten packs of strings, some picks, and that’s my final offer.”
He goes back and comes back, and he goes, “Okay, you got a deal.” I was having a party, as you can imagine.
Humans aren’t rationale. That’s the way life works. Close the deal with a little extra win for yourself if
possible.
If the other person feels like they chose that they had to do a little work, a lot of times, they’re more satisfied.
I’m not saying to take advantage of people, but when they have to do that extra little work, they go, “Okay,
good.” Any questions from this last section?

Whatever they’re being paid is the market rate for them at that time. As an example, we might have someone

There’s a phenomenon that within about 30 to 90 days, anyone who gets paid any amount of money higher,

They’re the ones that are doing all of the day-to-day work, and so they see challenges and obstacles, and most

So you have to keep pulling everybody out. You have to keep pulling them up to the next level. Zooming

So don’t underestimate the power of helping people see the bigger picture. Never underestimate that power

Now, to keep your business going and growing and really on the fast track, I think you need to maintain a
high energy and intensity, and you need to maintain a rhythm. So energy plus rhythm – if you don’t have
energy, you just have rhythm, you’re not going to be going anywhere very quickly. And if you have high
energy, but you have no rhythm, you’re not going to be focused. You’re not going to be directed.
So the energy and the rhythm, in my experience, comes from a combination of the entire group getting
together and doing what I call linking and syncing, so linking up with each other, connecting, and then
syncing. And then stopping to focus and reflect on what you’ve gotten done and what you’re trying to
accomplish.
I think it was Nietzsche that said – I’m paraphrasing now, but the average person lives in a world of complete
uncertainty and insecurity, and they’re constantly trying to orient themselves in this world of uncertainty.
We’re trying to orient ourselves, so in other words, as we’re driving, we want to turn on the GPS system in
the car and see where we’re at. You know if you’re on a long trip, and you’ve got a map – you’re using an oldschool printed map. You’re always pulling it out and looking at – okay, how far did we get? Where are we at?
We want to reorient ourselves all the time.
In relationships, we’re always saying and doing little things to kind of ping the other person and to kind of
say, where are we at? I want to orient myself to – can I still be secure? Do you still love me? Do we still have
trust? So we’re constantly trying to orient and reorient ourselves in a world of total uncertainty.
So linking and syncing, connecting, reorienting, zooming out – this is key. And there are two processes that I
recommend that you do every day in your business. If you will install these two processes and do them every
day with yourself and with your team, if you have others that you work with, I think that you can really make
a lot of progress.
The first one is a daily meeting or what Verne Harnish calls a “daily huddle.” You might want to check out his

128

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 9
book, which is Mastering the Rockefeller Habits by Verne Harnish. Mastering the Rockefeller Habits. H-AR-N-I-S-H, and he talks about daily huddles.
Now, we’ve done these for years. We did them in the beginning of the business, and we go through cycles,
and now basically, we do them across the whole business. Daily meeting – every day. Ideally, early in the day
get the whole team together in person, if you work in an office or on the phone if you don’t. And it’s a short
meeting. This should be ten, 15 minutes. If things are heavy, maybe it’s 20 or 30 minutes, but short meeting.
Short meeting, and the purpose is for everyone to chime in and give an update of their progress.
And what this does is it brings everyone together. You always know where to find everyone. You’re never
more than several hours away from connecting with everyone. You don’t have to be, “I don’t know where that
person is,” and maybe I’ll talk to him later in the week. It’s, “I know where they’re gonna be. They’re gonna be
at the daily meeting. They’re gonna be in the daily huddle, and I’m gonna be able to connect with them then.”
It also creates kind of a sense of energy and urgency for everyone because they all know they’re gonna show
up the next day, and they know that they’re gonna have to talk about what they’re up to and what they’re
doing.
Now, in our business, what I’ve learned is that I’m kind of the quarterback in a way. Let’s say we got a –
right now, we’ve got a team of about ten people. We’re doing a lot of product launches. We’re creating new

129

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 9
So there are two magic words that you’re gonna want to use in these conversations with your team. One of
them is “weeds.” I learned this from Ralph on our team. We’re getting into the weeds, meaning we’re getting
tactical, and we’re having a conversation that isn’t serving the whole group. You don’t want to get into the
weeds in a quick, daily huddle. You don’t want to do the work. You’re just linking and syncing and seeing
what conversations need to be had. So if it’s longer than that little 15 or 30 second conversation, you or the
organizer or both of you says, “Weeds.”
And the other magic phrase is “take it off line.” And what that reminds everyone to do is – this is a
conversation that needs to be had, but it needs to be had after the call or after the meeting. So take it off line.
The meeting is to figure out which conversations need to be had and where there are sticking points and
where there are updates to make sure everybody’s rocking and rolling. You follow me?
This is vital if you want to grow fast. It’s really vital. When I get into a – when we get into any kind of pattern
of doing something new and we need to get focused, the first thing that happens is – is the daily meeting
right? Is it happening at the right time? Have we stopped doing it?
Have we moved to – sometimes we get into a pattern where we were doing daily meetings, and we launched
a whole bunch of products, and it’s time to take a deep breath and relax for a few weeks. And a couple of
people get some time off because it was really hardcore, and then we come back, and we’re gonna get back
into the next cycle. It’s let’s get that daily meeting back together. It’s critical. That’s the starting place.
Recently, I realized I was kind of off track a little bit, and I said to Rob Scott here – we had talked about it. He
was doing some personal coaching and teaching some other people about it. And I emailed him, and I said,
“Hey Rob, who’s the best coach you know? Could you give me a referral because I want to talk to someone.”
He goes, “Me.” And I said, “That’s a pretty good answer.”
And so he started out doing some coaching with me, and I said, “I want to talk to you every day during the
week.” It’s actually four days a week. And I don’t want to talk about anything fancy. I want to talk about my
sleep, my exercise and my food. I just want to get those things locked down – daily basis.
The daily huddle is huge in your personal and your business life because it just keeps bringing you back to
what you’re supposed to be doing – brings everybody back.
So that’s the bookend at the front of the day. At the end of the day – at the back of the day, the other bookend
is the daily update. This is very important for your team members. Get everyone on your team to do a daily
update, and make it very nonthreatening. Say this is what I’d like you to do. At the end of every day – not
at the beginning of the next and not at the middle of the day, but at the end of the day when you’re done
working, I’d like you to take five minutes and update the group on what you did that day. I’d like you to tell us
three things with a couple of optional ones.
No. 1 – what I worked on today, results I got, what I accomplished. So what I worked on, results I
accomplished, what I got done. It’s important to talk about results like things that got done to keep the
mind focused on outcomes. Not work – it’s not, “I worked on this,” but here’s what I got done. Here’s what I
delivered.
No. 2 – problems and challenges. Were there any obstacles that came up?
And then No. 3 – questions that you have. Do you have any questions?

130

GURU MasterClass Management Summit Session 9
If you start with a daily huddle and you end with a daily update, and you use those two as the bookends, you’ll
find that somehow that’s like rocket fuel for your spaceship. It’s something the culturally just puts you on a
track to get moving.
Now some people say, “Oh, meetings. I don’t want to add more meetings.” This isn’t a meeting. This is
something that’s not like a meeting. This is not where you’re sitting around bored like Dilbert cartoon or
something. Doing something – wasting time. This is high energy, and you might even make it like a 12minute meeting.
Our team has adopted some weird times like one of our daily huddles – we get on at 11:02 a.m. just because
it’s weird. And I think that they’re getting off of a 12-minute huddle call or something. So short – 10, 12 or
15 minutes. Just going to get together and link and sync – quick update on what everybody’s doing – what
everyone accomplished. What everyone’s up to today, and then at the end of the day, daily update. Focuses
the mind, focuses the team.
An optional afterwards – if you’re working on a lot of things you’re juggling, have each person put a list of
their priorities of what their priorities are at the end of their daily update. It can be very, very useful to see
what they think the priorities are because then you can come back and say, “You know what? Move this one
around. Work on this.” And you can help organize better.
And also, I think that the daily update and the daily huddle and the priorities – it really allows the individual
to kind of have control of their world a little bit more because now they’re – they know what they’re doing,
and everybody knows what they’re doing, and it’s transparent. And they’re just getting the job done.
So instead of micromanaging – you’re always calling them up going, “What are you doing? What are you
working on?” It’s obvious. It’s apparent what they’re working on. You hear them talking about it every day.
In Mastering the Rockefeller Habits, Verne Harnish talks about how the Ritz Carleton – at the corporate
offices, the CEO and all 80 people that work at the corporate office get together in a room everyday for a tenminute daily huddle. And they talk about what’s going on every day. Interesting – the Ritz.

And if you said to me starting a business from scratch right now, and you’ve got a new team of people, these
would be the first things that I would do. First structural things – daily huddle and daily updates.
And here’s something that you’ll notice – when you say to the team, “Create daily updates. Do it at the end of
the day. Takes five minutes. Put three things in there.” Whatever – you’ll see how people behave. You will
get a feeling for who they are and how consistent they are and how disciplined they are.
When you’re hiring someone new, daily update from day one. Critical that you have them do a daily update
from day one. In fact, it’s probably the most important thing that you can do when you hire a new person
is to sit down with them and say, “Here’s what I’d like you to do. Every day, at the end of the day, take five
minutes and send me a daily update by email. If it takes more than five minutes, you’re doing something
wrong. Don’t take more than five, maybe ten minutes if it’s a complex one. I just want you to tell me what
did you work one. What did you accomplish? Problems, challenges, questions you have for me and maybe
priorities if you’re at that point.”
The reason why you want to do this is at the end of the first 30 days of working with someone, you’ll have a
feel for who they are. You’ll learn so much from this that – until you’ve done it, you can imagine how valuable
it will be. The people that have done it after hearing it from me and use it religiously report back that it’s
been very valuable. Anyone using these and finding them really valuable with your team? Okay, good.
It’s so simple. It’s so simple. If you go to your team now and do it, you will probably encounter resistance,
and that’s okay. It’s okay. Some people kind of feel like, “Well, I don’t want to be micromanaged and this and
that.” Or if you go and you say, “You know what? We’re gonna do a daily huddle.” Another meeting every
day? Ahh.
If you do a daily huddle and daily update, this takes, realistically, maybe 30 minutes of someone’s eighthour day, you’ll find that it will probably eliminate more other meetings and wasted time and hassle. It
will probably save more time than it costs to do it, plus you get the value of doing it. Plus you get the
synchronization.

Most of us don’t live in a mechanistic business. It’s not we’re doing the same exact thing every single day.
The priorities are changing. The projects are changing. In the information product world, we’re selling
